Generators of the Algebraic Symplectic Bordism Ring Thanks: The author was partially supported by the DFG through the grant LE 2259/8-1 and by the ERC through the project QUADAG. This paper is part of a project that has received funding from the European Research Council (ERC) under the European Union’s Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme (grant agreement No. 832833).
Pietro Gigli

Abstract
. In this paper, we study the η \eta -completed part of the motivic spectrum MSp constructed by Panin and Walter, representing the universal Sp {\operatorname{Sp}} -oriented cohomology theory. In particular, we investigate the inclusion ( MSp η ∧ ) ∗ ↪ MGL ∗ ({\operatorname{MSp}}^{\wedge}_{\eta})^{*}\hookrightarrow{\operatorname{MGL}}^{*} of the cofficient rings, by studying the motivic Adams spectral sequence associated to MSp, mimiking a strategy used by Levine,Yang, Zhao for MSL ∗ {\operatorname{MSL}}^{*} . In order to give a description of ( MSp η ∧ ) ∗ ({\operatorname{MSp}}^{\wedge}_{\eta})^{*} , we refine the Pontryagin-Thom construction in a way that allows one to obtain symplectic bordism classes from a large family of varieties that carry a certain "symplectic twist", and we prove a criterion to select generators among these classes.



1.
Introduction

Motivation

We work over a perfect field
k
of characteristic not 2, and consider cohomology theories over smooth
k
-schemes. The main goal of this paper is to obtain a partial computation of the coefficient ring of algebraic symplectic cobordism, the universal symplectically oriented cohomology theory for schemes. A complete description of this ring is at present unknown.

The strongest notion of "orientation" for a cohomology theory is that of
{\operatorname{GL}}
-orientation, which corresponds to the data of Thom classes for vector bundles, with appropriate compatibilities among them. Voevodsky’s algebraic cobordism spectrum
{\operatorname{MGL}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(k)

[
47
, Section 6.3]
is the most natural analogue of the complex cobordism spectrum
{\operatorname{MU}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}

[
7
, Chapter 1, Section 5]
in our setting, and has a natural
{\operatorname{GL}}
-orientation by construction. Just as
{\operatorname{MU}}
represents the universal complex oriented cohomology theory (
[
35
, Lemma 4.1.13]
), similarly
{\operatorname{MGL}}
is universal among
{\operatorname{GL}}
-oriented theories, namely, every
{\operatorname{GL}}
-oriented cohomology theory
{\mathcal{E}}^{*,*}(-)
is identified by a map
{\operatorname{MGL}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
of commutative ring spectra in
{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
. Hoyois
[
16
]
and Spitzweck
[
43
]
showed that, after inverting
{\operatorname{char}}k
if
{\operatorname{char}}k\neq 0
, the algebraic diagonal
{\operatorname{MGL}}^{*}:={\operatorname{MGL}}^{2*,*}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)
of the coefficient ring of
{\operatorname{MGL}}
is isomorphic to the Lazard ring
{\mathbb{L}}
, the universal coefficient ring for formal group laws, which is known to be the polynomial ring in infinite variables
{\mathbb{Z}}[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots]

[
1
, Part II, Theorem 7.1]
. Moreover, through a Pontryagin-Thom construction, one can associate to each smooth proper variety
X
over
k
a class
[X]_{\operatorname{MGL}}\in{\operatorname{MGL}}^{-{\operatorname{dim}}X}
(see
[
22
, Definition 3.1]
), and
{\operatorname{MGL}}^{*}
is generated by such classes (
[
22
, Theorem 3.4 (4)]
). In summary, we have the following.

Theorem 1
.

Let
p
be the exponential characteristic of
k
, namely
p={\operatorname{char}}k
if
{\operatorname{char}}k>0
and
p=1
otherwise. Then we have an isomorphism of graded ring

{\operatorname{MGL}}^{*}[1/p]\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}[1/p][x_{1},x_{2},\ldots]

with
x_{i}
in degree
-i
, and
{\operatorname{MGL}}^{*}[1/p]
is generated by classes
[X]_{\operatorname{MGL}}
,
X
a smooth and proper
k
-scheme.

Nevertheless, in
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-homotopy theory various "interesting" cohomology theories are not
{\operatorname{GL}}
-oriented, examples include Chow-Witt theory and Hermitian
K
-theory. This led to defining weaker notions of orientation (
[
31
]
,
[
33
]
), which consists of a theory of Thom classes for vector bundles with additional structures instead of all vector bundles.

Panin and Walter
[
30
, Section 6]
constructed the motivic spectrum
{\operatorname{MSp}}
representing algebraic symplectic cobordism, a motivic analogue of quaternionic symplectic cobordism
{\operatorname{MSp}}^{\operatorname{top}}
(
[
27
]
,
[
7
, Chapter 1, Section 5]
), and proved that this is universal for having an orientation for symplectic vector bundles
[
30
, Theorem 4.5]
. The above mentioned Pontryagin-Thom construction can be adapted to construct a class
[X]_{\operatorname{MSp}}\in{\operatorname{MSp}}^{-{\operatorname{dim}}X}
for each smooth proper
k
-variety
X
with a stably symplectic structure on the tangent bundle. Knowing the complete structure of the coefficient ring
{\operatorname{MSp}}^{*,*}
would give useful insights in understanding
{\operatorname{Sp}}
-orientations and formal ternary laws. It is then natural to ask whether
{\operatorname{MSp}}^{*}
has a description similar to that of
{\operatorname{MGL}}^{*}
given by Theorem
1
. It turns out that for
{\operatorname{MSp}}
the picture is much less clear, and in fact the structure of the coefficient ring of
{\operatorname{MSp}}
is mostly unknown. We can immediately make a couple of observations:

1)
({\operatorname{MSp}}^{\operatorname{top}})^{*}
is not polynomial (see e.g.
[
37
]
). Roughly, this arises from the fact that the sum of a non-trivial symplectic bundle with itself can be a trivial symplectic bundle, and this gives indecomposable torsion elements of order 2. For example, Ray
[
36
]
gave explicit computations of indecomposable torsion elements
\phi_{i}\in({\operatorname{MSp}}^{\operatorname{top}})^{-8i+3}
of order
2
for all
i\geq 1
. Based on the situation in classical homotopy theory, we expect that
{\operatorname{MSp}}^{*}
is also not polynomial and contains infinitely many torsion elements.

2) If we want to compute generators of
{\operatorname{MSp}}^{*}
through the Pontryagin-Thom construction, we realize that there are not many known examples of symplectic varieties in algebraic geometry. Standard examples like
K3
-surfaces and their Hilbert schemes of points are computationally difficult to handle, compared to projective spaces and Milnor hypersurfaces in the
{\operatorname{GL}}
-oriented case.

A key fact that needs to be considered to simplify the problem, is that the category of motivic spectra
{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
, after inverting
2
, has a splitting

{\operatorname{SH}}(k)[1/2]\simeq{\operatorname{SH}}(k)^{-}\oplus{\operatorname{SH}}(k)^{+}

induced by the involution
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\wedge{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\xrightarrow{1:1}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\wedge{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
,
and so does the coefficient ring of
{\operatorname{MSp}}
. A special role in this splitting is played by the motivic Hopf map
\eta:{\mathbb{G}}_{m}\to 1_{k}
, inasmuch the projection
{\operatorname{SH}}(k)[1/2]\to{\operatorname{SH}}(k)^{-}
corresponds to localizing at
\eta
and the projection
{\operatorname{SH}}(k)[1/2]\to{\operatorname{SH}}(k)^{+}
corresponds to a certain
\eta
-completion
(-)_{\eta}^{\wedge}
. Bachmann and Hopkins studied the
\eta
-inverted symplectic bordism ring, establishing the relation
{\operatorname{MSp}}[1/\eta]^{*}\cong W(k)[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots]
, where
W(k)
is the Witt ring of
k
, and
x_{i}
is in degree
2i
(
[
4
, Theorem 8.7]
). This takes care of the minus-part of
{\operatorname{MSp}}[1/2]^{*}
.

In this paper, we investigate in details the plus-part of
{\operatorname{MSp}}[1/2]^{*}
. As in topology, the main tool for this purpose is the Adams spectral sequence. Nevertheless, the algebraic Adams spectral sequence differs significantly from its topological analogue, one of the main differences being the non-nilpotence of the motivic Hopf map
\eta
.

Strategy and final results

The inclusion
{\operatorname{Sp}}_{*}\subset{\operatorname{GL}}_{*}
induces a natural map
\Phi:{\operatorname{MSp}}\to{\operatorname{MGL}}
, which in turn induces the map
({\operatorname{MSp}}^{\wedge}_{\eta})^{*}\to({\operatorname{MGL}}^{\wedge}_{\eta})^{*}={\operatorname{MGL}}^{*}
,
{\operatorname{MGL}}
being
\eta
-completed. We want to prove that
\Phi_{*}
is a split embedding. We face this problem by fixing an odd prime
\ell
and comparing the mod-
\ell
Adams spectral sequences associated to the two spectra. We adopt the strategy used in
[
22
]
to prove that the Adams spectral sequence for
{\operatorname{MSp}}
has the form

E_{2}^{s,t,u}={\operatorname{Ext}}_{A^{*,*}}^{s,(t-s,u)}(H^{*,*}({\operatorname{MSp}}),H^{*,*})\Rightarrow({\operatorname{MSp}}_{H{\mathbb{Z}}/\ell}^{\wedge})^{t,u},

and it degenerates at the second page (Propositions
5.20
-
5.21
), analogously to that of
{\operatorname{MGL}}
. We figure a presentation of the
E_{2}
-page as a polynomial
Z/\ell
-algebra, and comparing this with the
E_{2}
-page of the spectral sequence for
{\operatorname{MGL}}
given by
[
22
, Proposition 5.7]
, we prove that
({\operatorname{MSp}}_{\eta,\ell}^{\wedge})^{2*}\cong({\operatorname{MGL}}_{\ell}^{\wedge})^{2*}
, while
({\operatorname{MSp}}_{\eta,\ell}^{\wedge})^{2*+1}=0
. In particular,
({\operatorname{MSp}}_{\eta,\ell}^{\wedge})^{*}
is polynomial over
{\mathbb{Z}}/\ell
and concentrated in non-positive even degrees.
Along the way, by using a known criterion to detect generators in
{\operatorname{MGL}}^{*}
, we explicitely compute elements
[Y_{2d}]_{\operatorname{MSp}}\in{\operatorname{MSp}}^{-2d}
, for
d\geq 1
, which generate
({\operatorname{MSp}}_{\eta,\ell}^{\wedge})^{*}
as subalgebra of
({\operatorname{MGL}}^{\wedge}_{\ell})^{*}
via
\Phi_{*}
.
Finally, by working on the
\ell
-torsion of
({\operatorname{MSp}}_{\eta,\ell}^{\wedge})^{*}
one prime
\ell
at a time, we globalize the previous results, and we conclude the following.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
({\overline{{\operatorname{MSp}}}^{\wedge}_{\eta}})^{*}[1/2p]
be the quotient of
({{\operatorname{MSp}}^{\wedge}_{\eta}})^{*}[1/2p]
by its maximal subgroup that is
\ell
-divisible for all odd primes
\ell\neq p
. Then
({\overline{{\operatorname{MSp}}}^{\wedge}_{\eta}})^{*}[1/2p]\cong{\mathbb{Z}}[1/2p][y_{1},y_{2},\ldots]
, with
y_{i}
in degree
-2i
. Moreover,
y^{\prime}\in{\overline{{\operatorname{MSp}}}^{\wedge}_{\eta}})^{-2d}[1/2p]
is a generator if and only if the following is satisfied:

c_{(2d)}(\Phi_{*}y^{\prime})=\begin{cases}\lambda\in{\mathbb{Z}}[1/2p]^{\times}&\text{for}\;\;2d\neq\ell^{r}-1,\;\;\text{if all prime}\;\ell\neq 2,p;\;\forall r\geq 1\\
\lambda^{\prime}\cdot\ell,\;\lambda^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}^{\times}&\text{if}\;\;2d=\ell^{r}-1,\;\;\ell\;\text{prime},\;\ell\neq 2,p;\;r\geq 1,\end{cases}

with
c_{(2d)}(y)
a certain characteristic number associated to universal Newton classes in motivic cohomology.

See Theorem
6.25
.

The strategy is completely parallel to that used by Levine, Yang and Zhao in
[
22
]
to compute the
\eta
-completed SL-cobordism ring
({\operatorname{MSL}}^{\wedge}_{\eta})^{*}
, but there are critical differences in our situation to take into account. First, some cellularity properties for
{\operatorname{MSp}}
and its motive
{\operatorname{MSp}}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}
are required in order to prove the convergence of the Adams spectral sequence. For
{\operatorname{MGL}}
and
{\operatorname{MSL}}
this cellularity is induced by the Schubert filtration on ordinary Grassmannians, but this can not be extended to our case due to the different geometry of quaternionic Grassmannians. We then need to study the geometry of quaternionic Grassmannians, following
[
33
]
, and use a double-inductive argument to get the required properties. This procedure was also used in
[
40
, Proposition 3.1]
. Also, we already mentioned that the application of the Pontryagin-Thom construction to compute classes in the symplectic bordism ring is not very helpful. We then use an isomorphism of Thom spaces due to Ananyevskiy (
[
2
, Lemma 4.1]
), which is also a special instance of
[
38
, Proposition 2.2]
, to extend the symplectic Pontryagin-Thom construction to varieties that are not necessarily symplectic but carry a certain "symplectic twist", namely varieties
X
for which the Thom space
{\operatorname{Th}}(T_{X})
of the tangent bundle is stably isomorphic to the Thom space of a virtual vector bundle admitting a virtual symplectic form. Lastly, in order to compute characteristic numbers of our new twisted classes, we need to modify the classical Voevodsky’s construction of the degree map in motivic cohomology in order to take the symplectic twist into account.

Organization of the paper

In §
2
we fix all the necessary background for our work. In particular, we start by recalling the basic notions from motivic homotopy theory and the formalism of six functors for the sake of the reader. We then review the theory of
{\operatorname{GL}}
-orientations and
{\operatorname{Sp}}
-orientations, including discussions on the universal motivic spectra
{\operatorname{MGL}}
and
{\operatorname{MSp}}
in order to fit all the needs for our future purposes. In §
3
we use Ananyveskiy’s isomorphism
{\operatorname{Th}}(E\oplus L)\simeq{\operatorname{Th}}(E\oplus L^{\vee})
to define a twisted version of Voevodsky’s degree map, which we use then to compute the degree of a certain kind of twisted cohomology classes valued in motivic cohomology. In §
4
we show that
{\operatorname{MSp}}
is a cellular spectrum and we prove a similar statement for the
H{\mathbb{Z}}
-module
{\operatorname{MSp}}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}
. In §
5
we study the convergence of the mod-
\ell
Adams spectral sequence for
{\operatorname{MSp}}
by using the main results of §
4
, and give a presentation of the
E_{2}
-page as a trigraded
{\mathbb{Z}}/\ell
-algebra. In §
6
we use the results of §
5
to prove that the
\eta
-completed symplectic bordism ring is a polynomial subalgebra of the algebraic bordism ring, ad we use the computations of §
3
to prove a criterion to detect polynomial generators by looking at some characteristic numbers.

Notations and conventions

All schemes in this paper are assumed to be quasi-compact quasi-separated. Starting from §
2.3
, we work over a perfect field
k
of exponential characteristic
p\neq 2
, and we write
{\mathcal{H}}_{\bullet}(k)\coloneqq{\mathcal{H}}_{\bullet}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)
,
{\operatorname{SH}}(k)\coloneqq{\operatorname{SH}}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)
for the unstable and stable motivic homotopy category over
k
. For
\mathcal{C}
Ab-enriched category, we write
[A,B]_{\mathcal{C}}
for the abelian group
{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,B)
, in particular we use this notation for cohomology groups. For
{\mathcal{E}}^{*,*}(-)
cohomology theory, we write
{\mathcal{E}}^{*,*}\coloneqq{\mathcal{E}}^{*,*}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)=\oplus_{a,b\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathcal{E}}^{a,b}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)
for the (bigraded) coefficient ring,
{\mathcal{E}}^{*}:={\mathcal{E}}^{2*,*}=\oplus_{a\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathcal{E}}^{2a,a}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)
for its (graded) diagonal, and
{\mathcal{E}}^{a}:={\mathcal{E}}^{2a,a}({\operatorname{Spec}}k).
For
F\dashv G
adjoint functors, we occasionally use the notation
\eta_{(F,G)}
and
\epsilon_{(F,G)}
for the counit and unit of the adjunction respectively. Starting from §
5
,
\ell
will be a fixed odd prime different from
p
,
\nu_{\ell}:{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
will denote the
\ell
-adic valuation, and
G^{\wedge}_{\ell}
will denote the
\ell
-adic completion of a (possibly graded) abelian group
G
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Background on
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-homotopy theory

Let
S
be a Noetherian scheme of finite Krull dimension. Let
{\operatorname{Sch}}/S
be the category of quasi-projective
S
-schemes, and
{\operatorname{Sm}}/S
the full subcategory of
{\operatorname{Sch}}/S
of smooth quasi-projective
S
-schemes.

We follow the classical approach of Morel and Voevodsky.
{\operatorname{Spc}}(S)
and
{\operatorname{Spc}}_{\bullet}(S)
denote respectively the categories of Nisnevich sheaves of simplicial sets and Nisnevich sheaves of pointed simplicial sets on
{\operatorname{Sm}}/S
. We call objects in
{\operatorname{Spc}}(S)
and
{\operatorname{Spc}}_{\bullet}(S)

spaces
and
pointed spaces
respectively. By identifying a scheme
U\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/S
with the Nisnevich sheaf associated to the constant representable presheaf
\text{Hom}_{{\operatorname{Sm}}/S}(-,U)
we get the embedding
{\operatorname{Sm}}/S\hookrightarrow{\operatorname{Spc}}(S)
, and similarly
({\operatorname{Sm}}/S)_{+}\hookrightarrow{\operatorname{Spc}}_{\bullet}(S)
. We also have the
smash product

\wedge
on
{\operatorname{Spc}}_{\bullet}(S)
.

By
[
47
, Theorem 3.7]
,
{\operatorname{Spc}}(S)
has a closed model structure, with
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-weak equivalences as in
[
47
, Definition 3.4]
. These weak equivalences are intended to be an algebraic analogue of homotopy equivalences of topological spaces. The resulting homotopy category is denoted by
{\mathcal{H}}(S)
.

The model structure on
{\operatorname{Spc}}(S)
induces a model structure on
{\operatorname{Spc}}_{\bullet}(S)
, and the resulting homotopy category is denoted by
{\mathcal{H}}_{\bullet}(S)
. We refer to objects in
{\mathcal{H}}_{\bullet}(S)
as
motivic spaces
.

Remark 2.1
.

{\operatorname{Spc}}(S)
and
{\operatorname{Spc}}_{\bullet}(S)
have all small limits and colimits. On the other hand, despite homotopy categories are not complete nor cocomplete in general, one can always take homotopy limits and homotopy colimits in
{\operatorname{Spc}}(S)
and
{\operatorname{Spc}}_{\bullet}(S)
as objects in
{\mathcal{H}}(S)
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{\bullet}(S)
respectively.

The smash product
\wedge
in
{\operatorname{Spc}}_{\bullet}(S)
induces a smash product
\wedge
in
{\mathcal{H}}_{\bullet}(S)
. With this product,
{\mathcal{H}}_{\bullet}(S)
acquires a symmetric monoidal structure, with unit being the
0
-sphere

S^{0}\coloneqq S_{+}
. This follows by the properties of weak equivalences (see
[
47
, Section 3, pp.585-586]
).

In
{\mathcal{H}}_{\bullet}(S)
, there are two "circles": the
simplicial circle

S^{1}
(see for instance
[
47
]
before lemma 3.8) and the
punctured line

{\mathbb{G}}_{m}\coloneqq({\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{S}\setminus\{0\},1)
. One defines then the
motivic spheres

S^{p,q}\coloneqq(S^{1})^{\wedge(p-q)}\wedge{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\wedge q}.

For
p\geq q\geq 0
, we have the suspension endofunctor
\Sigma^{p,q}:{\mathcal{H}}_{\bullet}(S)\to{\mathcal{H}}_{\bullet}(S)
defined by
\Sigma^{p,q}X\coloneqq S^{p,q}\wedge X
.

There is a canonical isomorphism
S^{2,1}\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{1}=:({\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{S},\infty)
in
{\mathcal{H}}_{\bullet}(S)
, given by the fact that both these spaces are homotopy pushouts of
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}\hookleftarrow{\mathbb{G}}_{m}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
in
{\operatorname{Spc}}_{\bullet}(S)
, with
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
pointed at
1
.

For
\pi:V\to S
vector bundle,
{\mathbb{P}}(V)
denotes the associated projective space
\operatorname{Proj}({\operatorname{Sym}}^{*}{\mathcal{V}})\xrightarrow{q}S
, with
{\mathcal{V}}
sheaf of sections of
V^{\vee}
. The
Thom space of
V
, denoted by
{\operatorname{Th}}(V)
, is the quotient
{\mathbb{P}}(V\oplus\mathcal{O}_{S})/{\mathbb{P}}(V)
in
{\operatorname{Spc}}_{\bullet}(S)
, with the obvious distinguished point, which exists because of Remark
2.1
. Equivalently,
{\operatorname{Th}}(V)
is the quotient
V/V-s_{0}(S)
, with
s_{0}:S\to V
the zero section.

Remark 2.2
.

(1)

From the properties of the smash product, it follows that, if
V_{1},V_{2}\to S
are two vector bundles over
S
, one has
{\operatorname{Th}}(V_{1}\oplus V_{2})\simeq{\operatorname{Th}}(V_{1})\wedge{\operatorname{Th}}(V_{2})
.

(2)

Since
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\simeq{\mathbb{A}}^{1}/{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
, in
{\mathcal{H}}_{\bullet}(S)
, we have in particular
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\simeq{\operatorname{Th}}(\mathcal{O}_{S})
in
{\mathcal{H}}_{\bullet}(S)
. By the previous point, one also gets
{\operatorname{Th}}(\mathcal{O}_{S}^{n})\simeq({\mathbb{P}}^{1})^{\wedge n}\simeq S^{2n,n}
. If
S\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/S^{\prime}
, we can consider
{\operatorname{Th}}(\mathcal{O}_{S}^{n})
as a motivic space over
S^{\prime}
. Technically, it corresponds to
S^{2n,n}\wedge S_{+}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\bullet}(S^{\prime})
.

(3)

Let
g:X\to Y
be a map in
{\operatorname{Sm}}/S
, and
V\to X
,
W\to Y
two vector bundles with a map
f:V\to W
arising from a fiberwise injective map
f_{X}:V\to g^{*}W
. Then the Thom space construction gives naturally a map
{\operatorname{Th}}(g,f):{\operatorname{Th}}_{X}(V)\to{\operatorname{Th}}_{Y}(W)
in
{\mathcal{H}}_{\bullet}(S)
. If the map
f
is obvious from the context, for instance if
V=g^{*}W
, we will just write
{\operatorname{Th}}(g)
.

There exist various equivalent stabilization procedures which produce the stable homotopy category
{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
. One model is given by
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
-spectra. In general, for
T\in{\operatorname{Spc}}_{\bullet}(S)
, a
T
-spectrum

{\mathbb{E}}
is a sequence of pointed spaces
(E_{0},E_{1},E_{2},\ldots)
with bonding maps
\epsilon_{r}:T\wedge E_{r}\to E_{r+1}
, and morphisms of
T
-spectra are componentwise morphisms of pointed spaces such that all the obvious squares with the bonding maps commute. There is also a notion of
stable weak equivalences
of
T
-spectra
[
47
, before Definition 5.1]
. The stable homotopy category
{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
is the left Bousfield localization of
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
-spectra with respect to these equivalences (see
[
47
, Definition 5.7]
,
[
20
, Theorem 2.9]
or
[
14
, Definition 3.3]
). Objects in
{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
are called
motivic
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
-spectra over
S
.

The category
{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
has again all small limits and colimits. There is an
infinite
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
-suspension functor

\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{\infty}:{\mathcal{H}}_{\bullet}(S)\to{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
defined by

\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{\infty}(X)\coloneqq(X,{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\wedge X,({\mathbb{P}}^{1})^{\wedge 2}\wedge X,\ldots,({\mathbb{P}}^{1})^{\wedge r}\wedge X,\ldots),

with bonding maps being the identities, and suspension endofunctors
\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}},\Sigma_{S^{1}},\Sigma_{{\mathbb{G}}_{m}}:{\operatorname{SH}}(S)\to{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
. The main features of
{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
are recalled in the following theorem.

Theorem 2.3

(
[
47
]
, Proposition 5.4, Theorem 5.6)
.

{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
is additive, with coproduct
\oplus
induced by the wedge sum of pointed spaces, and has a triangulated structure with shift functor being
\Sigma_{S^{1}}
. Also,
{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
is symmetric monoidal, with monoidal product
\wedge
induced by the smash product of pointed spaces, and monoidal unit the sphere spectrum
1_{S}\coloneqq\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{\infty}S_{+}
, and the following properties hold:

(1)

For
{\mathbb{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
and
X\in{\operatorname{Spc}}_{\bullet}(S)
, the motivic spectrum
{\mathbb{E}}\wedge\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{\infty}X
is canonically isomorphic to the spectrum
(E_{0}\wedge X,E_{1}\wedge X,E_{2}\wedge X,\ldots)
with bonding maps
\epsilon_{i}\wedge{\operatorname{id}}_{X}
.

(2)

For
{\mathbb{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
and
\{{\mathbb{F}}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha}
a collection of motivic spectra in
{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
, we have a canonical isomorphism

(\oplus_{\alpha}{\mathbb{F}}_{\alpha})\wedge{\mathbb{E}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\oplus_{\alpha}({\mathbb{F}}_{\alpha}\wedge{\mathbb{E}}).

Basically by construction of
{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
and Property (1) of Theorem
2.3
,
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
is invertible in
{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
, then
\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}},\Sigma_{S^{1}},\Sigma_{{\mathbb{G}}_{m}}
are invertible endofunctors. One can then define
\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{n}
,
\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{n}
,
\Sigma_{{\mathbb{G}}_{m}}^{n}
for any
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. By adopting a notation analogous to the unstable motivic spheres, one writes
\Sigma^{p,q}\coloneqq\Sigma_{S^{1}}^{p-q}\circ\Sigma_{{\mathbb{G}}_{m}}^{q}
for all
p,q
in
{\mathbb{Z}}
, and we have
\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}\simeq\Sigma^{2,1}
.

Every motivic spectrum
{\mathbb{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
defines bigraded cohomology functors
{\mathbb{E}}^{p,q}(-):{\operatorname{Spc}}(S)^{\text{op}}\to\text{Ab}
and homology functors
{\mathbb{E}}_{p,q}(-):{\operatorname{Spc}}(S)\to\text{Ab}
for any integer
p,q
, in the category of abelian groups, through:

{\mathbb{E}}^{p,q}(X)\coloneqq[\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{\infty}X_{+},\Sigma^{p,q}{\mathbb{E}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(S)},\;\;\,{\mathbb{E}}_{p,q}(X)\coloneqq[1_{S},\Sigma^{-p,-q}{\mathbb{E}}\wedge\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{\infty}X_{+}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(S)}.

For
f:X\to Y
in
{\operatorname{Spc}}(S)
, composing with
\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{\infty}f
defines the ordinary cohomology pullback
{\mathbb{E}}^{p,q}(f)=f^{*}
and the ordinary homology pushforward
{\mathbb{E}}_{p,q}(f)=f_{*}
. Let us note that
{\mathbb{E}}^{p,q}(S)={\mathbb{E}}_{-p,-q}(S)
.

Definition 2.4
.

A commutative monoid object
{\mathcal{E}}
in the symmetric monoidal category
{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
is called a
motivic commutative ring spectrum
.

In particular, a motivic commutative ring spectrum
{\mathcal{E}}
is equipped with a multiplication map
\mu_{{\mathcal{E}}}:{\mathcal{E}}\wedge{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
and a unit map
\epsilon_{\mathcal{E}}:1_{S}\to{\mathcal{E}}
.

For
{\mathcal{E}}
motivic commutative ring spectrum, the multiplication map
\mu_{\mathcal{E}}
induces a natural cup product
\cup:{\mathcal{E}}^{p,q}(X)\times{\mathcal{E}}^{p^{\prime},q^{\prime}}(X)\to{\mathcal{E}}^{p+p^{\prime},q+q^{\prime}}(X)
, which makes
{\mathcal{E}}^{*,*}(X)
a graded ring, and a natural cap product
\cap:{\mathcal{E}}^{p,q}(X)\times{\mathcal{E}}_{p_{1},q_{1}}(X)\to{\mathcal{E}}_{p_{1}-p,q_{1}-q}(X)
. Also, for
{\mathbb{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
, we have a canonical pairing
\langle-,-\rangle:{\mathcal{E}}^{p,q}({\mathbb{E}})\times{\mathcal{E}}_{p_{1},q_{1}}({\mathbb{E}})\to{\mathcal{E}}_{p_{1}-p,q_{1}-q}(S)
defined as

(2.1)

\langle\alpha,\gamma\rangle:1_{S}\xrightarrow{\gamma}\Sigma^{-p_{1},-q_{1}}{\mathcal{E}}\wedge{\mathbb{E}}\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{id}}_{\mathcal{E}}\wedge\alpha}\Sigma^{p-p_{1},q-q_{1}}{\mathcal{E}}\wedge{\mathcal{E}}\xrightarrow{\mu_{\mathcal{E}}}\Sigma^{p-p_{1},q-q_{1}}{\mathcal{E}}.

Remark 2.5
.

If
{\mathcal{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
is a commutative ring spectrum, then for any
{\mathbb{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
we have the
motivic
{\mathcal{E}}
-Hurewicz map

h_{\mathcal{E}}:{\mathbb{E}}_{a,b}(S)\to{\mathcal{E}}_{a,b}({\mathbb{E}})
defined by
h_{\mathcal{E}}(f):=(\epsilon_{\mathcal{E}}\wedge{\operatorname{id}})\circ f:1_{S}\to\Sigma^{-a,-b}{\mathcal{E}}\wedge{\mathbb{E}}.
For
\alpha\in{\mathcal{E}}^{p,q}({\mathbb{E}})
,
\gamma_{0}\in{\mathbb{E}}_{p_{1},q_{1}}(S)
, we then have
\langle\alpha,h_{\mathcal{E}}(\gamma_{0})\rangle=\alpha\circ\gamma_{0}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{p_{1}-p,q_{1}-q}(S)
.

Note that, if
X\in{\operatorname{Spc}}(S)
has structure map
p
, the cohomology pullback
p^{*}
makes the coefficient ring
{\mathcal{E}}^{*,*}(X)
a bigraded module over
{\mathcal{E}}^{*,*}(S)
. The
{\mathcal{E}}
-cohomology
{\mathcal{E}}^{*,*}(-)
can then be seen as a functor from
{\operatorname{Spc}}(S)^{\operatorname{op}}
to bigraded
{\mathcal{E}}^{*,*}(S)
-algebras.

There exist various standard models for
{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
as homotopy category of some symmetric monoidal model category. One such model is given by symmetric spectra (see
[
20
]
or
[
15
]
).

Definition 2.6
.

A motivic commutative ring spectrum
{\mathcal{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
is said to be a
highly structured motivic commmutative ring spectrum
if it admits a model
{\mathcal{E}}^{{\operatorname{hs}}}
as a commutative monoid object in a certain symmetric monoidal model category
{\mathcal{S}\text{p}}^{{\operatorname{hs}}}_{S}
such that
\text{Ho}({\mathcal{S}\text{p}}^{{\operatorname{hs}}}_{S})\simeq{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
as symmetric monoidal categories. For
{\mathcal{E}}
a highly structured motivic commutative ring spectrum with model
{\mathcal{E}}^{{\operatorname{hs}}}\in{\mathcal{S}\text{p}}^{\operatorname{hs}}_{S}
, the homotopy category
\text{Ho}({\operatorname{Mod}}_{{\mathcal{E}}^{{\operatorname{hs}}}})
of
{\mathcal{E}}^{{\operatorname{hs}}}
-module objects in
{\mathcal{S}\text{p}}^{{\operatorname{hs}}}_{S}
gives the
category of
{\mathcal{E}}
-modules

{\operatorname{Mod}}_{\mathcal{E}}
. Moreover, the usual free-forget adjunction on
{\mathcal{E}}^{{\operatorname{hs}}}
-modules in
{\mathcal{S}\text{p}}^{\operatorname{hs}}_{S}
induces the adjunction

\text{Free}_{\mathcal{E}}:{\operatorname{SH}}(S)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{\operatorname{Mod}}_{\mathcal{E}}:\text{Forget},

where, for a motivic spectrum
{\mathbb{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
,
\text{Free}_{\mathcal{E}}({\mathbb{E}})
is the
{\mathcal{E}}
-module
{\mathbb{E}}\wedge{\mathcal{E}}
with module map
{\operatorname{id}}_{\mathbb{E}}\wedge\mu_{\mathcal{E}}:{\mathbb{E}}\wedge{\mathcal{E}}\wedge{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathbb{E}}\wedge{\mathcal{E}}
(we use the right modules notation).

Example 2.7
.

Voevodsky’s
Eilenberg-Maclane spectrum

H{\mathbb{Z}}
is the motivic spectrum representing
motivic cohomology
. Over a field of characteristic
0
, the construction is described in
[
47
, Section 6.1]
, but the definitions has been extended over more general bases in many ways (see
[
39
]
,
[
18
]
,
[
42
]
,
[
6
]
,
[
17
]
).
H{\mathbb{Z}}
is a highly structured motivic ring spectrum. This is proved for instance in
[
11
, Example 3.4]
through a different model for
H{\mathbb{Z}}
, which is nevertheless equivalent to Voevodsky’s Eilenberg-Maclane spectrum by
[
11
, Lemma 4.6]
.

This allows one to define the category
{\operatorname{Mod}}_{H{\mathbb{Z}}}
of
H{\mathbb{Z}}
-modules. For
S={\operatorname{Spec}}k
,
k
a field, we let
{\operatorname{DM}}(k)\coloneqq{\operatorname{Mod}}_{H{\mathbb{Z}}}
. Usually,
{\operatorname{DM}}(k)
denotes Voevodsky’s triangulated category of motives constructed in
[
50
]
, but the main result of
[
39
]
shows that this is equivalent to
{\operatorname{Mod}}_{H{\mathbb{Z}}}
in characteristic
0
, and in characteristic
p
the same is true after inverting
p
, by
[
18
, Theorem 5.8]
. So for our purposes in this work there should be no confusion.

We also recall a partial computation of motivic cohomology.

Theorem 2.8

(
[
24
]
, Theorem 19.1, Theorem 19.3, Corollary 4.2)
.

For
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
, with
k
a perfect field,
H{\mathbb{Z}}^{p,q}(X)=0
if either
q<0
,
p>2q
or
p>q+{\operatorname{dim}}(X)
. Moreover,
H{\mathbb{Z}}^{0,0}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)={\mathbb{Z}}
,
H{\mathbb{Z}}^{p,0}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)=0
for
p\neq 0
, and
H{\mathbb{Z}}^{p,1}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)=0
for
p\neq 1
.

2.2.
The six functors

As
S
varies among quasi-compact quasi separated schemes over a fixed base scheme
B
Noetherian of finite Krull dimension, the categories
{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
acquire the formalism of Grothendieck’s six operations, developed in
[
3
]
. We now recall the main features.

Definition/Proposition 2.9
.

For every morphism
f:S\to T
of schemes, we have two adjoint functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}:{\operatorname{SH}}(T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{SH}}(S):f_{*},}

where
f^{*}
is symmetric monoidal and compatible with the unstable functor
f^{*}
through infinite
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
-suspension functors.
f^{*}
and
f_{*}
are called, respectively,
inverse image along
f
and
direct image along
f
. Furthermore, if
f
is separated of finite presentation, we have another pair of adjoint functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{!}:{\operatorname{SH}}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{SH}}(T):f^{!},}

called, respectively,
exceptional direct image along
f
and
exceptional inverse image along
f
.

In almost all cases in this paper, we will work on
{\operatorname{Sch}}/B
, and morphisms of quasi-projective schemes are automatically separated of finite presentation. Then we will usually not worry about the existence of the exceptional functors.

Sending
S\in{\operatorname{Sch}}/B
to
{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
, and a map of schemes
f
to the functor
f^{*}
, defines a pseudofunctor

{\operatorname{SH}}(-):{\operatorname{Sch}}/B^{\text{op}}\to\mathbf{Tr}^{\otimes}

from
{\operatorname{Sch}}/B
to the category of symmetric monoidal triangulated categories. From now on, for the sake of the exposition, we will focus on the case
S\in{\operatorname{Sch}}/B
. Differently, the statements involving
f_{!}
and
f^{!}
only hold for
f
separated of finite presentation.

The construction of the functors
f^{*},f_{*},f^{!},f_{!}
is discussed in
[
3
, Chapter 1]
, as are the properties listed in the following proposition.

Proposition 2.10
.

The four functors for
{\operatorname{SH}}(-)
defined in Proposition
2.9
satisfy the following properties:

(1)

For every
f
, there is a natural transformation
f_{!}\to f_{*}
that is invertible if
f
is proper.

(2)

If
f
is an open immersion, there is a natural isomorphism
f^{*}\xrightarrow{\sim}f^{!}
.

(3)

If
f
is smooth,
f^{*}
has a left adjoint, denoted by
f_{\#}
.

(4)

(Projection formula) The exceptional direct image satisfies projection formula against the inverse image. Namely, for
f:S\to T
,
{\mathbb{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
,
{\mathbb{F}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(T)
, there is a canonical isomorphism in
{\operatorname{SH}}(T)

{\mathbb{F}}\wedge f_{!}{\mathbb{E}}\xrightarrow{\sim}f_{!}(f^{*}{\mathbb{F}}\wedge{\mathbb{E}}).

(5)

(Base change) For a cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

there are canonical natural isomorphisms
g^{*}f_{!}\xrightarrow{\sim}f^{\prime}_{!}g^{\prime*}
and
g^{\prime}_{*}f^{\prime!}\xrightarrow{\sim}f^{!}g_{*}.

In addition to this, the
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-invariance property of
{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
can be stated in terms of the four functors as follows.

Proposition 2.11

(
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-invariance)
.

If
\pi:E\to X
is a vector bundle,
\pi^{*}
is fully faithful. In particular, the maps
\epsilon_{(\pi^{*},\pi_{*})}:{\operatorname{id}}_{{\operatorname{SH}}(X)}\to\pi_{*}\pi^{*}
and
\eta_{(\pi_{\#},\pi^{*})}:\pi_{\#}\pi^{*}\to{\operatorname{id}}_{{\operatorname{SH}}(X)}
are invertible.

Definition 2.12
.

The family of functors
(f^{*},f_{*},f_{!},f^{!})
, together with the monoidal product bifunctor
\wedge
and the internal-Hom bifunctor
\underline{{\operatorname{Hom}}}
, are called the
six functors
for
{\operatorname{SH}}(-)
.

Remark 2.13
.

Let
p:X\to S
a smooth map. Working unstably, one easily sees that
p_{\#}(({\operatorname{id}}_{X})_{+})=(X\xrightarrow{p}S)_{+}=X_{+}
. Thus, in
{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
,
p_{\#}1_{X}=p_{\#}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}({\operatorname{id}}_{X})_{+})\simeq\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{\infty}X_{+}
.

We also mention a well known reformulation of Morel-Voevodsky localization theorem in the language of six functors. For a proof see for instance
[
3
, Corollary 3.2.3]
.

Proposition 2.14
.

Let
i:Z\to S
be a closed immersion, with complementary open immersion
j:U\to S
. Then we have the distinguished triangles

i_{!}i^{!}\xrightarrow{\eta_{(i_{!},i^{!})}}{\operatorname{id}}_{{\operatorname{SH}}(S)}\xrightarrow{\epsilon_{(j^{*},j_{*})}}j_{*}j^{*}\to i_{!}i^{!}[1]\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;j_{!}j^{!}\xrightarrow{\eta_{(j_{!},j^{!})}}{\operatorname{id}}_{{\operatorname{SH}}(S)}\xrightarrow{\epsilon_{(i^{*},i_{*})}}i_{*}i^{*}\to j_{!}j^{!}[1]

of endofunctors of
{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
, called the localization sequences.

Let now
\pi:V\to S
be a vector bundle, with zero section
s_{0}:X\hookrightarrow V
. Then the adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{V}\coloneqq\pi_{\#}s_{0!}:{\operatorname{SH}}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{SH}}(S):s_{0}^{!}\pi^{*}=:\Sigma^{-V}}

is a self-equivalence of
{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
.

Definition 2.15
.

The endofuntors
\Sigma^{V}
and
\Sigma^{-V}
are called the
V
-suspension
and the
V
-desuspension
respectively. Often, one calls them generically the
Thom equivalences
.

All the results about Thom equivalences that we are going to state here are discussed in
[
3
, Section 1.5]
and recalled in
[
8
, Section 2.1]
.

Thom equivalences are compatible with the monoidal structure, in the sense that, for
V\to S
vector bundle, and
{\mathbb{E}},{\mathbb{F}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
, one has
\Sigma^{V}{\mathbb{E}}\wedge{\mathbb{F}}\simeq\Sigma^{V}({\mathbb{E}}\wedge{\mathbb{F}})
, and analogously for
\Sigma^{-V}
. Then, the functor
\Sigma^{V}(-)
can be read as
\Sigma^{V}1_{S}\wedge(-)
, and the same for
\Sigma^{-V}(-)
. We also have canonical isomorphisms

(2.2)

f^{*}\Sigma^{V}\simeq\Sigma^{f^{*}V}f^{*},\;\;\Sigma^{V}f_{*}\simeq f_{*}\Sigma^{f^{*}V},\;\;\Sigma^{V}f_{!}\simeq f_{!}\Sigma^{f^{*}V},\;\;f^{!}\Sigma^{V}\simeq\Sigma^{f^{*}V}f^{!}.

Let now
K(S)
be the Thomason-Trobaugh
K
-theory space of perfect complexes on
S
(
[
46
, Definition 3.1]
), and let
{\mathcal{K}}(S)
denote the fundamental groupoid of
K(S)
. Let
{\mathcal{V}}
be a locally free sheaf of finite rank over
S
, and let us consider the associated vector bundle
V={\mathbb{V}}({\mathcal{V}})\coloneqq{\operatorname{Spec}}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}{\operatorname{Sym}}^{*}({\mathcal{V}})
. Again, we have endofunctors of
\Sigma^{\mathcal{V}}\coloneqq\Sigma^{V}
and
\Sigma^{-{\mathcal{V}}}\coloneqq\Sigma^{-V}
of
{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
. The assignment
{\mathcal{V}}\to\Sigma^{\mathcal{V}}
naturally extends to a functor of groupoids
\Sigma^{(-)}:{\mathcal{K}}(S)\to{\operatorname{Aut}}({\operatorname{SH}}(S))
, where
{\operatorname{Aut}}({\operatorname{SH}}(S))
is the groupoid of autoequivalences of
{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
with their natural isomorphisms as morphisms.

Remark 2.16
.

Let us note that a distinguished triangle
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime\prime}\to{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}[1]
of perfect complexes on
S
induces canonically an identification
{\mathcal{V}}\simeq{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}+{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime\prime}
in
{\mathcal{K}}(S)
, inducing the isomorphism
\Sigma^{\mathcal{V}}\simeq\Sigma^{{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}+{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime\prime}}\simeq\Sigma^{{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}}\circ\Sigma^{{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime\prime}}.

In particular, this gives the isomorphism

(2.3)

\Sigma^{\mathcal{V}}1_{S}\simeq\Sigma^{{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}}1_{S}\wedge\Sigma^{{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime\prime}}1_{S}.

The canonical distinguished triangles
{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{V}}\oplus{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{V}}[1]
and
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{V}}\oplus{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}[1]
give canonical isomorphisms
\Sigma^{\mathcal{V}}\circ\Sigma^{{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}}\simeq\Sigma^{{\mathcal{V}}\oplus{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}}\simeq\Sigma^{{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}}\circ\Sigma^{\mathcal{V}}
. Similarly, the distinguished triangle
{\mathcal{V}}\to 0\to{\mathcal{V}}[1]\to{\mathcal{V}}[1]
gives the canonical isomorphism
\Sigma^{{\mathcal{V}}}\circ\Sigma^{{\mathcal{V}}[1]}\simeq\Sigma^{0}={\operatorname{id}}
, so, for
{\mathcal{V}}
a locally free sheaf, we have
\Sigma^{-{\mathcal{V}}}=(\Sigma^{{\mathcal{V}}})^{-1}=\Sigma^{{\mathcal{V}}[1]}
. We can thus extend the notation
\Sigma^{\mathcal{V}}
to virtual perfect complexes by setting
\Sigma^{{\mathcal{V}}-{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}}:=\Sigma^{{\mathcal{V}}}\circ\Sigma^{{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}[1]}
.

The Morel-Voevodsky relative purity theorem
[
26
, §3 Theorem 2.23]
has a reformulation in the stable setting in terms of six functors:

Theorem 2.17

(
[
8
]
, item 2.1.8)
.

Let
i:Z\hookrightarrow X
be a closed immersion of smooth schemes over
S
locally of finite type, with structure maps
p_{Z}
,
p_{X}
. Then there are natural isomorphisms

p_{X\#}i_{*}\simeq p_{Z\#}\Sigma^{N_{i}}\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;\Sigma^{-N_{i}}p_{Z}^{*}\simeq i^{!}p_{X}^{*},

where
N_{i}
is the normal bundle of
i
.

On the other hand, Ayoub’s purity theorem
[
3
, Section 1.6]
implies the following:

Theorem 2.18

(
[
8
]
, item 2.1.7)
.

If
f
is a smooth morphism locally of finite type, there is a canonical isomorphism

(2.4)

\Sigma^{T_{f}}f^{*}\xrightarrow{\sim}f^{!},

where
T_{f}
is the relative tangent bundle of
f
.

Remark 2.19
.

If
f
is a smooth morphism, taking the left adjoints of both sides of the natural isomorphism (
2.4
) gives the natural isomorphism

f_{\#}\Sigma^{-T_{f}}\simeq f_{!}.

Remark 2.20
.

Proposition
2.10
(4) and Remark
2.19
imply that, for
f
smooth,
f_{\#}
satisfies a projection formula against
f^{*}
. Also,
f_{\#}
verifies the same compatibility as
f_{!}
in (
2.2
) with Thom equivalences, since Thom equivalences commute with each other.

Remark 2.21
.

If
f
is étale,
T_{f}
is the zero bundle
S\to S
, so
\Sigma^{T_{f}}={\operatorname{id}}_{{\operatorname{SH}}(S)}
. Thus, Theorem
2.18
gives an isomorphism
f^{*}\xrightarrow{\sim}f^{!}
. If
f
is an open immersion, this is the isomorphism of Proposition
2.10
(2) . Moreover, in this case we also have
f_{\#}\simeq f_{!}
by Remark
2.19
.

For
\pi:V\to S
a vector bundle, let
j:V^{0}\to V
be the open complement of the zero section
s_{0}:S\to V
. By Proposition
2.14
, we have a distinguished triangle

(2.5)

j_{!}j^{!}1_{V}\to 1_{V}\to s_{0*}s_{0}^{*}1_{V}\to j_{!}j!1_{V}[1].

Let
\pi^{0}:V^{0}\to S
be the restriction of
\pi
. By applying
\pi_{\#}
to (
2.5
), and using
j_{!}\simeq j_{\#}
from Remark
2.21
and
j^{!}\simeq j^{*}
from Proposition
2.10
(2), we obtain the distinguished triangle

(2.6)

\pi^{0}_{\#}1_{V^{0}}\to\pi_{\#}1_{V}\to\pi_{\#}s_{0*}1_{S}\simeq\Sigma^{V}1_{S}\to\pi^{0}_{\#}1_{V^{0}}[1],

where the isomorphism follows because
s_{0*}\simeq s_{0!}
by Proposition
2.10
(1). This can be rewritten as

(2.7)

\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{\infty}V_{+}^{0}\to\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{\infty}V_{+}\to\Sigma^{V}1_{S}\to\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{\infty}V_{+}^{0}[1],

from which we see that
\Sigma^{V}1_{S}\simeq\Sigma^{\infty}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}{\operatorname{Th}}(V)
. For this reason, the motivic spectrum
\Sigma^{V}1_{S}
is also called the
stable Thom space of
V
.

In view of this discussion on stable Thom spaces, we can say that the relation (
2.3
) is a generalization of Remark
2.2
(1).

Remark 2.22
.

By recalling Remark
2.2
(2), we have
\Sigma^{\mathcal{O}_{S}}\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\wedge(-)\simeq\Sigma^{2,1}
as endofunctors of
{\operatorname{SH}}(S)
. By taking wedge powers of
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, one also gets
\Sigma^{\mathcal{O}_{S}^{r}}\simeq\Sigma^{2r,r}
.

2.3.
{\operatorname{GL}}
-oriented theories

From now on, we focus on the case
S={\operatorname{Spec}}k
, with
k
perfect field of exponential characteristic
p\neq 2
.

The definition of a
{\operatorname{GL}}
-orientation is due to Panin
[
28
, Definition 3.1]
. In what follows, we adopt the conventions used in
[
2
]
.

Definition 2.23
.

A
{\operatorname{GL}}
-orientation
for a commutative ring spectrum
{\mathcal{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
consists of the assignment of Thom classes
{\operatorname{th}}^{\mathcal{E}}(V)\in{\mathcal{E}}^{2r,r}({\operatorname{Th}}(V))=[p_{\#}\Sigma^{V}1_{X},\Sigma^{2r,r}{\mathcal{E}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(k)}
for each vector bundle
V\to X
,
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
, with
r={\operatorname{rank}}(V)
, satisfying the following axioms:

(2.8)

(1)

Normalization: For
V={\mathbb{V}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{r})
over some smooth scheme
p:X\to{\operatorname{Spec}}k
,
{\operatorname{th}}^{\mathcal{E}}(V)\in{\mathcal{E}}^{2r,r}({\operatorname{Th}}(V))
is the image of the unit
1_{{\mathcal{E}}^{0,0}(X)}
under the suspension isomorphism
{\mathcal{E}}^{0,0}(X)={\mathcal{E}}^{0,0}(p_{\#}1_{X})\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathcal{E}}^{2r,r}(p_{\#}\Sigma^{2r,r}1_{X})={\mathcal{E}}^{2r,r}({\operatorname{Th}}(V))
.

(2)

Naturality: Given vector bundles
V\to X
,
W\to Y
,
X,Y\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
, a morphism
f:Y\to X
and an isomorphism
\alpha:W\xrightarrow{\sim}f^{*}V
of vector bundles on
Y
, let
{\operatorname{Th}}(\alpha,f):{\operatorname{Th}}(W)\to{\operatorname{Th}}(V)
be the obvious induced morphism of motivic spaces. Then

{\operatorname{Th}}(\alpha,f)^{*}({\operatorname{th}}^{\mathcal{E}}(V))={\operatorname{th}}^{\mathcal{E}}(W).

(3)

Multiplicativity: Given an exact sequence of vector bundles over
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k

0\to V^{\prime}\to V\to V^{\prime\prime}\to 0

of ranks
r^{\prime},r,r^{\prime\prime}
, respectively, one has the corresponding cup product map
\cup:{\mathcal{E}}^{2r^{\prime},r^{\prime}}({\operatorname{Th}}(V^{\prime}))\otimes{\mathcal{E}}^{2r^{\prime\prime},r^{\prime\prime}}({\operatorname{Th}}(V^{\prime\prime}))\to{\mathcal{E}}^{2r,r}({\operatorname{Th}}(V))
thanks to the relation (
2.3
). Then

{\operatorname{th}}^{\mathcal{E}}(V)={\operatorname{th}}^{\mathcal{E}}(V^{\prime})\cup{\operatorname{th}}^{\mathcal{E}}(V^{\prime\prime}).

Let
V\to X
be a rank
r
vector bundle on
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
, with structure morphism
p:X\to{\operatorname{Spec}}k
, and let
{\mathcal{V}}
be the sheaf of sections of
V^{\vee}
. Since
\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{\infty}{\operatorname{Th}}(V)\simeq p_{\#}(\Sigma^{\mathcal{V}}1_{X})
, we may use the adjunction
p_{\#}\dashv p^{*}
to view
{\operatorname{th}}^{\mathcal{E}}(V)
as an element
{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{V}})\in{\operatorname{Map}}_{{\operatorname{SH}}(X)}(\Sigma^{{\mathcal{V}}-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{r}}1_{X},p^{*}{\mathcal{E}})
.

Lemma 2.24
.

The assignment
{\operatorname{LocFree}}(X)\ni{\mathcal{V}}\mapsto{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{V}})\in{\operatorname{Map}}_{{\operatorname{SH}}(X)}(\Sigma^{{\mathcal{V}}-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{r}}1_{X},p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}),
with
r={\operatorname{rank}}{\mathcal{V}}
, extends to a natural transformation of functors of groupoids from
{\mathcal{K}}(X)
to
{\operatorname{SH}}(X)_{\simeq}

[{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}(-):(\Sigma^{(-)-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(-)}}1_{X})\to c_{p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}}]:{\mathcal{K}}(X)\to{\operatorname{SH}}(X)_{\simeq},

where
{\operatorname{SH}}(X)_{\simeq}
is the underlying groupoid of
{\operatorname{SH}}(X)
, and
c_{p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}}
is the constant functor with value
p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}
.

Proof.

The functor
\Sigma^{(-)}:{\mathcal{K}}(X)\to{\operatorname{Aut}}({\operatorname{SH}}(X))
gives rise to a functor of groupoids

(2.9)

\Sigma^{(-)-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(-)}}1_{X}:{\mathcal{K}}(X)\to{\operatorname{SH}}(X)_{\simeq}.

Composing (
2.9
) with the canonical functor of groupoids
i:{\operatorname{LocFree}}(X)\to{\mathcal{K}}(X)
, we get a functor of groupoids
{\operatorname{LocFree}}(X)\to{\operatorname{SH}}(X)_{\simeq}
. Let
\alpha:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{V}}
be an isomorphism in
{\operatorname{LocFree}}(X)
, giving the induced isomorphism
{\operatorname{Th}}(\alpha):\Sigma^{{\mathcal{W}}-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}({\mathcal{W}})}}1_{X}\to\Sigma^{{\mathcal{V}}-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}({\mathcal{V}})}}1_{X}
. By (
2.8
)(2), we see that
{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{V}})\circ{\operatorname{th}}(\alpha)={\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{W}})
, and we can then see the assignment
{\operatorname{LocFree}}(X)\ni{\mathcal{V}}\mapsto{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{V}})
as a natural transformation of functors of groupoids

(2.10)

[{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}(-):(\Sigma^{(-)-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(-)}}1_{X})\circ i\to c_{p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}}]:{\operatorname{LocFree}}(X)\to{\operatorname{SH}}(X)_{\simeq},

from the functor
(\Sigma^{(-)-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(-)}}1_{X})\circ i
to the constant functor
c_{p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}}
with value
p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}
.

If
q:\tilde{X}\to X
is a morphism in
{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
, the two functors commute with
q^{*}
. Thus, taking
q
as a Jouanolou cover of
X
, we can assume that
X
is affine.

If
{\mathcal{V}}\in{\operatorname{LocFree}}(X)
, with
X
affine, we have a canonical isomorphism

{\mathcal{V}}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{r}-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}({\mathcal{V}}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{r})}\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathcal{V}}-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}({\mathcal{V}})}

in
{\mathcal{K}}(X)
, which gives the corresponding isomorphism in
{\operatorname{SH}}(X)
after applying
\Sigma^{(-)}1_{X}
. We then have a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{{\mathcal{V}}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{r}-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}({\mathcal{V}}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{r})}}1_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{{\mathcal{V}}-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}({\mathcal{V}})}}1_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{V}}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{r})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{V}})}

It follows by (
2.8
)(1,3) that this diagram commutes. Thus, we can extend
{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}(-)
to differences
{\mathcal{V}}-\mathcal{O}_{X}^{r}
by letting
{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{V}}-\mathcal{O}_{X}^{r})\coloneqq{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{V}})
.

We recall from
[
13
]
that the groupoid
{\mathcal{K}}(X)
is obtained from the groupoid
{\operatorname{LocFree}}(X)
by inverting the autoequivalence
\oplus\mathcal{O}_{X}
. Therefore, we can extend the natural transformation (
2.10
) to a natural transformation

[{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}(-):(\Sigma^{(-)-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(-)}}1_{X})\to c_{p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}}]:{\mathcal{K}}(X)\to{\operatorname{SH}}(X)_{\simeq}.

∎

The natural transformation
{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}(-)
is multiplicative in the sense that, given
v,v^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)
, we have a canonical isomorphism

\Sigma^{v+v^{\prime}-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(v+v^{\prime})}}1_{X}\simeq\Sigma^{v-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{\text{rank}(v)}}1_{X}\wedge_{X}\Sigma^{v^{\prime}-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(v^{\prime})}}1_{X}.

Lemma 2.25
.

For
v,v^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)
,
{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}(v+v^{\prime})
is the composition

(2.11)

\Sigma^{v+v^{\prime}-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(v+v^{\prime})}}1_{X}\simeq\Sigma^{v-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(v)}}1_{X}\wedge_{X}\Sigma^{v^{\prime}-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(v^{\prime})}}1_{X}\\
\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}(v)\wedge{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}(v^{\prime})}p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\wedge_{X}p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\xrightarrow{\mu_{p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}}}p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}

where
\mu_{p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}}
is the multiplication on
p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}
.

The proof is a direct consequence of the multiplicativity of Thom classes (
2.8
)(3).

Definition 2.26
.

For
v\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)
,
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
, let
{\operatorname{th}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}^{f}(v):\Sigma^{v-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(v)}}p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\to p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}
be the composition

\Sigma^{v-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(v)}}p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\simeq\Sigma^{v-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(v)}}1_{X}\wedge_{X}p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}(v)\wedge{\operatorname{id}}_{p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}}}p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\wedge_{X}p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\xrightarrow{\mu_{p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}}}p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}.

Also, for
{\mathcal{E}}
a highly structured motivic commutative ring spectrum, let
{\operatorname{th}}^{f}_{{\mathcal{E}}{\operatorname{Mod}}}(v):\Sigma^{v-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(v)}}p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\to p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}
denote the map in
{\operatorname{Mod}}_{p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}}
arising from the morphism
{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}(v)
in
{\operatorname{SH}}(X)
by the Free-Forgetful adjunction between
{\operatorname{SH}}(X)
and
{\operatorname{Mod}}_{p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}}
.

Applying the forgetful functor to
{\operatorname{th}}^{f}_{{\mathcal{E}}{\operatorname{Mod}}}(v)
gives back the map
{\operatorname{th}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}^{f}(v)
, and for arbitrary
{\mathcal{E}}
, we recover
{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}(v)
from
{\operatorname{th}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}^{f}(v)
by composing with the unit
\Sigma^{v-\mathcal{O}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(v)}}1_{p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}^{0,0}(X)}
.

Lemma 2.27
.

For
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
,
v\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)
,
{\mathcal{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
oriented, the map
{\operatorname{th}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}^{f}(v):\Sigma^{v-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{rnk(v)}}p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\to p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}
in
{\operatorname{SH}}(X)
is an isomorphism in
{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
, and for
{\mathcal{E}}
highly structured,
{\operatorname{th}}^{f}_{{\mathcal{E}}{\operatorname{Mod}}}(v)
is an isomorphism in
{\operatorname{Mod}}_{p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}}
.

Proof.

Since the forgetful functor
{\operatorname{Mod}}_{p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}}\to{\operatorname{SH}}(X)
reflects isomorphisms, it suffices to see that
{\operatorname{th}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}^{f}(v)
is an isomorphism in
{\operatorname{SH}}(X)
. It follows from the normalization axiom (
2.8
)(1) that for
v=[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{r}]
,
{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}(v):1_{X}\to p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}
is the unit in
{\mathcal{E}}^{0,0}(X)
, which easily implies the lemma for
v=[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{r}]
. In general, we take a Zariski open cover
{\mathcal{U}}=\{U_{i}\}_{i}
for
X
for which the restriction of
v
to each
U_{i}
is
r_{i}[{\mathcal{O}}_{U_{i}}]
for some integer
r_{i}
. The result then follows from the gluing axiom for the sheaf
{\operatorname{SH}}(-)
on
X_{\operatorname{Zar}}
.
∎

Analogously to
{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}(-)
,
{\operatorname{th}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}^{f}(-)
can be viewed as a natural isomorphism of functors of groupoids from
{\mathcal{K}}(X)
to
{\operatorname{SH}}(X)_{\simeq}.

Proposition 2.28
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
be oriented and take
X\in{\operatorname{Sch}}/k
.

1. The functor of groupoids
\Sigma^{(-)}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}:{\mathcal{K}}(X)\to{\operatorname{SH}}(X)_{\simeq}
given by
v\mapsto\Sigma^{v}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}
descends to a map

\Sigma^{(-)}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}:K_{0}(X)\to{\operatorname{Obj}}({\operatorname{SH}}(X)),\ v\mapsto\Sigma^{v}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}.

2. The natural isomorphism
[{\operatorname{th}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}^{f}(-):(\Sigma^{(-)-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(-)}}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}})\to c_{p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}}]:{\mathcal{K}}(X)\to{\operatorname{SH}}(X)_{\simeq}
descends to a natural isomorphism

[{\operatorname{th}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}^{f}(-):(\Sigma^{(-)-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(-)}}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\to c_{p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}}]:K_{0}(X)\to{\operatorname{SH}}(X)_{\simeq}.

Proof.

(2) follows directly from (1).

For (1), let
\phi:v\to v^{\prime}
be an isomorphism in
{\mathcal{K}}(X)
, let
r={\operatorname{rank}}(v)={\operatorname{rank}}(v^{\prime})
and let
\Sigma^{\phi}(p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}):\Sigma^{v}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\simeq\Sigma^{v^{\prime}}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}
be the isomorphism induced from
\Sigma^{\phi}:\Sigma^{v}\simeq\Sigma^{v^{\prime}}
. This gives us the commutative diagram of isomorphisms in
{\operatorname{SH}}(X)

\textstyle{\Sigma^{v-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{r}}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{r}}\Sigma^{\phi}(p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}})}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{th}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}^{f}(v)}
\textstyle{p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{v^{\prime}-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{r}}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{th}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}^{f}(v^{\prime})}

In other words
\Sigma^{\phi}(p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}})=\Sigma^{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{r}}({\operatorname{th}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}^{f}(v)^{-1}\circ{\operatorname{th}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}^{f}(v))
. This shows that
\Sigma^{\phi}(p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}})
is independent of the choice of
\phi:v\to v^{\prime}
, proving (1).
∎

For
V_{1},V_{2}
vector bundles on
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
, we have the Thom class
{\operatorname{th}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}^{f}(V_{1}-V_{2})
defined as
{\operatorname{th}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}^{f}([{\mathcal{V}}_{1}]-[{\mathcal{V}}_{2}])
, where
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
is the sheaf of sections of
V_{i}^{\vee}
.

Lemma 2.29
.

Let
V_{1}\to X
,
V_{2}\to X
vector bundles on
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
of rank
r_{1},r_{2}
, and let
{\mathcal{V}}_{1},{\mathcal{V}}_{2}
be the respective locally free sheaves of sections of
V_{1}^{\vee},V_{2}^{\vee}
. Then
{\operatorname{th}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}^{f}(V_{1}-V_{2})\in[\Sigma^{[{\mathcal{V}}_{1}]-[{\mathcal{V}}_{2}]-(r_{1}-r_{2})[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]}p^{*}{\mathcal{E}},p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(X)}
is the composition

\Sigma^{[{\mathcal{V}}_{1}]-[{\mathcal{V}}_{2}]-(r_{1}-r_{2})[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]}p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\xrightarrow{\Sigma^{r_{2}[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]-[{\mathcal{V}}_{2}]}{\operatorname{th}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}^{f}(V_{1})}\Sigma^{-[{\mathcal{V}}_{2}]+r_{2}[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]}p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\\
\xrightarrow{(\Sigma^{-[{\mathcal{V}}_{2}]+r_{2}[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]}{\operatorname{th}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}^{f}(V_{2}))^{-1}}p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}

In particular, for
V\to X
a vector bundle of rank
r
, we have

{\operatorname{th}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}^{f}(-V)=(\Sigma^{-[{\mathcal{V}}]+r[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]}{\operatorname{th}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}^{f}(V))^{-1}.

The proof is a direct computation.

Definition 2.30
.

(1)

Let
f:X\to Y
a proper map in
{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
, and
p_{X},p_{Y}
the structure maps of the schemes. Since
f_{*}\simeq f_{!}
by Proposition
2.10
(1), we have the natural transformation

p_{Y!}\xrightarrow{p_{Y!}\epsilon_{(f^{*},f_{*})}}p_{Y!}f_{*}f*\simeq p_{Y!}f_{!}f^{*}=p_{X!}f^{*}.

Applying this to
1_{Y}
gives the
dual map
of
f
in
{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
:

f^{\vee}:p_{Y!}1_{Y}\to p_{X!}1_{X}.

(2)

In the same setting as (1), we suppose
X,Y
have dimension
d_{X},d_{Y}
respectively, and
f
has relative dimension
d
. Let
{\mathcal{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(k)

{\operatorname{GL}}
-oriented. The dual map
f^{\vee}
gives rise to a pullback
(f^{\vee})^{*}:{\mathcal{E}}^{a,b}(p_{X!}1_{X})\to{\mathcal{E}}^{a,b}(p_{Y!}1_{Y})
, and Remark
2.19
gives
p_{X!}\simeq p_{X\#}\Sigma^{-T_{p_{X}}}=p_{X\#}\Sigma^{-T_{X}}
. Then, for all
a,b
in
{\mathbb{Z}}
, we have

{\mathcal{E}}^{a,b}(p_{X!}1_{X})=[p_{X!}1_{X},\Sigma^{a,b}{\mathcal{E}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(k)}\cong[p_{X\#}\Sigma^{-T_{X}}1_{X},\Sigma^{a,b}{\mathcal{E}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(k)}\cong[1_{X},\Sigma^{T_{X}}p_{X}^{*}\Sigma^{a,b}{\mathcal{E}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(X)}\\
\cong[1_{X},\Sigma^{a,b}\Sigma^{T_{X}}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(X)}\xrightarrow[\sim]{\circ\Sigma^{a,b}{\operatorname{th}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}^{f}(T_{X})}[1_{X},\Sigma^{a,b}\Sigma^{2d_{X},d_{X}}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(X)}\\
\cong[1_{X},p_{X}^{*}\Sigma^{a+2d_{X},b+d_{X}}{\mathcal{E}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(X)}\cong[p_{X\#}1_{X},\Sigma^{a+2d_{X},b+d_{X}}{\mathcal{E}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(k)}={\mathcal{E}}^{a+2d_{X},b+d_{X}}(X).

In the same way, we have
{\mathcal{E}}^{a,b}(p_{Y!}1_{Y})\cong{\mathcal{E}}^{a+2d_{Y},b+d_{Y}}(Y).
Thus, by reindexing, we have that
(f^{\vee})^{*}
induced the
proper pushforward for
{\mathcal{E}}
along
f
:

f_{*}:{\mathcal{E}}^{a,b}(X)\to{\mathcal{E}}^{a-2d,b-d}(Y).

It is a standard computation to verify the following compatibilities between pullback and proper pushforward:

Proposition 2.31
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
be an oriented commutative ring spectrum.

1.(Push-pull formula) Let

\textstyle{Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{X}

be a transverse cartesian square, with
X,X^{\prime},Y,Y^{\prime}
in
{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
and with
f
proper. Then
f^{\prime}
is also proper and
g^{*}f_{*}=f^{\prime}_{*}g^{\prime*}
.

2. (Projection formula) Let
f:Y\to X
be a proper map in
{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
. Then for
x\in{\mathcal{E}}^{*,*}(X)
,
y\in{\mathcal{E}}^{*^{\prime},*^{\prime}}(Y)
, we have
f_{*}(f^{*}(x)\cdot y)=x\cdot f_{*}(y)
.

Definition 2.32
.

For
Y\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
of dimension
d
over
k
, a
stable twist of
-T_{Y}
is a pair
(v,\vartheta)
, where
v
is a virtual vector bundle of virtual rank
-d
, and
\vartheta
is an isomorphism
\Sigma^{-T_{Y}}1_{Y}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Sigma^{v}1_{Y}
in
{\operatorname{SH}}(Y)
.

Definition 2.33
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
be an oriented commutative ring spectrum. For
p_{Y}:Y\to{\operatorname{Spec}}k\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
proper of dimension
d
over
k
, let
(v,\vartheta)
be a stable twist of
-T_{Y}
. Let
{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}(v)^{\prime}:\Sigma^{2d,d}p_{Y\#}\Sigma^{v}1_{Y}\to{\mathcal{E}}
be the map corresponding to
{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}(v):\Sigma^{2d,d}\Sigma^{v}1_{Y}\to p_{Y}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}
by adjunction. We define the class
[Y,v,\vartheta]_{\mathcal{E}}\in{\mathcal{E}}^{-2d,-d}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)
as the composition

1_{k}\xrightarrow{p_{Y}^{\vee}}p_{Y\#}\Sigma^{-T_{Y}}1_{Y}\xrightarrow{p_{Y\#}(\vartheta)}p_{Y\#}\Sigma^{v}1_{Y}\xrightarrow{\Sigma^{-2d,-d}{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}(v)^{\prime}}\Sigma^{-2d,-d}{\mathcal{E}},

where
p_{Y}^{\vee}
is the dual map of
p_{Y}
of Definition
2.30
(1) composed with the purity isomorphism
p_{Y!}1_{Y}\simeq p_{Y\#}\Sigma^{-T_{Y}}1_{Y}
(Remark
2.19
). We use the shorthand
[Y]_{\mathcal{E}}:=[Y,-T_{Y},{\operatorname{id}}]_{\mathcal{E}}
.

Definition 2.34
.

For
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
proper of dimension
d_{X}
and structure map
p_{X}
, we have the
degree map
for motivic cohomology
{\operatorname{deg}}_{k}:H{\mathbb{Z}}^{2d_{X},d_{X}}(X)\to{\mathbb{Z}}
, defined by the proper pushforward
p_{X*}
, since
H{\mathbb{Z}}^{0,0}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)={\mathbb{Z}}
by Theorem
2.8
.

Chern classes

For
{\mathcal{E}}

{\operatorname{GL}}
-oriented,
V\to X
rank
r
vector bundle, X
\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
, one has Chern classes
c_{i}(V)\in{\mathcal{E}}^{2i,i}(X)
,
i=1,\ldots,r
satisfying the standard axioms, see
[
28
, Definition 3.26]
. For
{\mathcal{V}}
the sheaf of sections of a vector bundle
V
, we define
c_{i}({\mathcal{V}})
to be
c_{i}(V)
.

For
i>0
, the Chern classes
c_{i}(V)
are nilpotent elements in the graded ring
\oplus_{n}{\mathcal{E}}^{2n,n}(X)
(see
[
28
, Theorem 3.27]
).

Panin has shown (see
[
28
, Lemma 3.33, Theorems 3.5, 3.27, 3.36]
) that, for a commutative ring spectrum
{\mathcal{E}}
, giving a theory of Chern classes is equivalent to giving a theory of Thom classes. Under this correspondence, given a vector bundle
V\to X
, the top Chern class
c_{{\operatorname{rank}}(V)}(V)\in{\mathcal{E}}^{2{{\operatorname{rank}}(V)},{{\operatorname{rank}}(V)}}(X)
is the pullback
z_{0}^{*}{\operatorname{th}}^{\mathcal{E}}(V)
of
{\operatorname{th}}^{\mathcal{E}}(V)
along the zero section
z_{0}:X_{+}\to{\operatorname{Th}}(V)
in
{\mathcal{H}}_{\bullet}(k)
. This yields in particular an expression for the unique non-trivial Chern class
c_{1}(L)\in{\mathcal{E}}^{2,1}(X)
for a line bundle
L\to X
. Also,
c_{{\operatorname{rank}}(V)}(V)
can also be described in the following terms (Panin showed this for line bundles but the argument extends to finite rank).

Lemma 2.35
.

Let
V\to X
be a rank
r
vector bundle on
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
, and let
{\mathcal{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
be an oriented ring spectrum. If
s_{0}:X\to V
is the zero section and
s:X\to V
an arbitrary section, then
c_{r}(V)=s^{*}(s_{0*}(1^{\mathcal{E}}_{X}))
.

The following is
[
28
, Theorem 3.9]
.

Theorem 2.36

(Projective Bundle Formula)
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
be an oriented commutative ring spectrum. Let
X
be in
{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
, and let
V\to X
be a rank
n+1
vector bundle on
X
. Let
{\mathbb{P}}(V)
denote the projective vector bundle
\operatorname{Proj}({\operatorname{Sym}}^{*}{\mathcal{V}})\xrightarrow{q}X
, with tautological quotient invertible sheaf
q^{*}{\mathcal{V}}\twoheadrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{V}(1)
. Let us write
\xi:=c_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{V}(1))\in{\mathcal{E}}^{2,1}(X)
. Then
{\mathcal{E}}^{*,*}({\mathbb{P}}(V))
is a free
{\mathcal{E}}^{*,*}(X)
-module, via the pullback
q^{*}
, with basis
1,\xi,\ldots,\xi^{n}
.

The lower Chern classes
c_{i}(V)\in{\mathcal{E}}^{2i,i}(X)
,
0<i<{{\operatorname{rank}}(V)}
, for a vector bundle
V\to X
are determined using Grothendieck’s formula

(2.12)

\xi^{{\operatorname{rank}}(V)}+\sum_{i=1}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(V)}(-1)^{i}c_{i}(V)\cdot\xi^{{\operatorname{rank}}(V)-i}=0;\quad\xi:=c_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{V}(1))\in{\mathcal{E}}^{2,1}({\mathbb{P}}(V)).

Combining Theorem
2.36
with the Grothendieck formula (
2.12
) gives a description of
{\mathcal{E}}^{*,*}({\mathbb{P}}(V))
as a
{\mathcal{E}}^{*,*}(X)
-algebra.

For any
{\operatorname{GL}}
-oriented theory
{\mathcal{E}}
we have an isomorphism of
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded rings
{\mathcal{E}}^{2*,*}(k)[[x_{1},x_{2}]]\cong{\mathcal{E}}^{2*,*}({\mathbb{P}}^{\infty}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{\infty})
, with
x_{i}
mapping to
p_{i}^{*}(c_{1}({\mathcal{O}}(1)))
, and via this isomorphism a uniquely defined
F=F(x_{1},x_{2})\in{\mathcal{E}}^{2*,*}(k)[[x_{1},x_{2}]]
such that
F(x_{1},x_{2})=c_{1}(x_{1}\otimes x_{2})
. Panin showed
[
28
, Proposition 3.37]
that given two line bundles
L_{1},L_{2}
on
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
, one has
c_{1}(L_{1}\otimes L_{2})=F(c_{1}(L_{1}),c_{1}(L_{2}))
, where the right-hand side makes sense because
c_{1}(L_{i})
are nilpotent, and by
[
28
, Proposition 3.38]

F(x,y)
is a rank one formal group law over
{\mathcal{E}}^{2*,*}(k)
.

Example 2.37
.

Motivic cohomology
H{\mathbb{Z}}
has a canonical orientation with additive formal group law
F(x,y)=x+y
. See for example
[
6
, Section 11.3]
.

Newton classes

For
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
and
V\to X
a rank
r
vector bundle, with associated flag bundle
\pi:\text{Fl}(V)\to X
, the pullback
\pi^{*}V
admits a canonical filtration

0=F^{r+1}V\subset F^{r}V\subset\ldots\subset F^{1}V\subset F^{0}V=\pi^{*}V

with subquotients
L_{i}:=F^{i}V/F^{i+1}V
all line bundles. For
{\mathcal{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
an oriented commutative ring spectrum, the
{\mathcal{E}}
-Chern roots
of
V
are the element
\xi_{i}^{\mathcal{E}}(V):=c_{1}^{\mathcal{E}}(L_{i})\in{\mathcal{E}}^{2,1}(\text{Fl}(V))
. If
{\mathcal{E}}=H{\mathbb{Z}}
, we write
\xi_{i}
for
\xi_{i}^{H{\mathbb{Z}}}
, and we call the classes
\xi_{i}(V)
simply the
Chern roots of
V
. The splitting principle tells us that
\pi^{*}:{\mathcal{E}}^{2*,*}(X)\to{\mathcal{E}}^{2*,*}(\text{Fl}(V))
is injective, with image the elements of the form
P(\xi^{\mathcal{E}}_{1},\ldots,\xi^{\mathcal{E}}_{r})
for
P\in{\mathcal{E}}^{2*,*}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]^{\Sigma_{r}}
a symmetric polynomial.

Definition 2.38
.

1. Let
m(x,t)
be the formal product
m(x,t):=\prod_{i,j=1}^{\infty}1+x_{i}^{j}t_{j}
, and
I:=(i_{1}\geq i_{2}\geq\ldots\geq i_{r}\geq 0)
a partition. We let
|I|:=\sum_{j}i_{j}
, and
t_{I}:=t_{i_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{r}}
. Writing
m(x,t)
as
\sum_{I}m_{I}(x)t_{I}
gives the
monomial symmetric function

m_{I}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots)
, which is a homogeneous polynomial in
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots
of degree
|I|
.

2. Let
{\mathcal{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
be an oriented commutative ring spectrum and
V\to X
a rank
r
vector bundle on
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
. For a partition
I
, the
I
-th Conner-Floyd Chern class

c_{I}^{\mathcal{E}}(V)\in{\mathcal{E}}^{2|I|,|I|}(X)
is the element corresponding to
m_{I}(\xi_{1}^{\mathcal{E}}(V),\ldots,\xi_{r}^{\mathcal{E}}(V),0,0,\ldots)
via the splitting principle. For
{\mathcal{E}}=H{\mathbb{Z}}
, we write simply
c_{I}(V)
.

3. We call the
n
-th Newton class of
V
the Conner-Floyd Chern class
c_{I}(V)
relative to the partition
I=(n)
, namely:

c_{(n)}(V)\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{r}\xi_{i}(V)^{n}\in H\mathbb{Z}^{2n,n}(X).

4. For a variety
X
, smooth and proper over
k
of dimension
n
,
s_{n}(X)
denotes the number

s_{n}(X)\coloneqq\text{deg}_{k}(c_{(n)}(T_{X}))\in\mathbb{Z}.

Lemma 2.39
.

(1)

If
V\to X
is a vector bundle on
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
, and
n>{\operatorname{dim}}_{k}X
, then

c_{(n)}(V)=0
.

(2)

Newton classes are additive: if
V_{1},V_{2}\to X
are two vector bundles, we have

c_{(n)}(V_{1}\oplus V_{2})=c_{(n)}(V_{1})+c_{(n)}(V_{2}).

(3)

Newton classes are natural: given
V\to X
a vector bundle and
f:Y\to X
a morphism in
{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
, we have
c_{(n)}(f^{*}(V))=f^{*}(c_{(n)}(V))
in
H{\mathbb{Z}}^{2n,n}(Y)
.

(4)

If
r\geq 2
and
X=\prod_{i=1}^{r}X_{i}
is the product of
r
smooth proper varieties, with
1\leq{\operatorname{dim}}_{k}X_{i}<n
for all
i
and
n
a positive integer, then
c_{(n)}(T_{X})=0
. In particular, the characteristic numbers
s_{n}
vanish on decomposable varieties.

Proof.

(1) follows from the fact that for
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
,
H\mathbb{Z}^{2n,n}(X)={\operatorname{CH}}^{n}(X)=0
for
n>{\operatorname{dim}}_{k}X
. (2) follows from the fact that if
V_{1}
has Chern roots
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{r}
and
V_{2}
has Chern roots
\xi_{r+1},\ldots,\xi_{r+s}
, then
V_{1}\oplus V_{2}
has Chern roots
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{r+s}
. (3) follows from the naturality of the Chern roots with respect to pullback.
(4) follows from the other three, since
T_{X}\simeq\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}p_{i}^{*}T_{X_{i}}
, with
p_{i}:X\to X_{i}
the projections.
∎

Remark 2.40
.

For a partition
I=i_{1}\geq i_{2}\geq\ldots\geq i_{s}\geq 0
, let
I^{\prime}=(i^{\prime}_{1},i^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{i_{1}},0,0,\ldots)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\infty}
be the tuple defined by
i^{\prime}_{m}=\#\{j\mid i_{j}=m\}
. This gives
t_{I}=t^{I^{\prime}}=\prod_{j=1}^{i_{1}}t_{j}^{i^{\prime}_{j}}
. The generating function
m(x,t)
can be rewritten as
m(x,t)=\sum_{I}m_{I}(x)t^{I^{\prime}}
. If we define
\langle I^{\prime}\rangle:=\sum_{i}j\cdot i^{\prime}_{j}
, we have
|I|=\langle I^{\prime}\rangle
. Given a vector bundle
V\to X
on
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
, we have the
Conner-Floyd Chern polynomial

c^{\operatorname{CF}}(V)=\sum_{I}c_{I}(V)t_{I}=\sum_{I}c_{I}(V)t^{I^{\prime}}.

For
0\to V^{\prime}\to V\to V^{\prime\prime}\to 0
an exact sequence of vector bundles on
X
, the Chern roots of
V
are those of
V^{\prime}
together with those of
V^{\prime\prime}
, which easily implies
c^{\operatorname{CF}}(V)=c^{\operatorname{CF}}(V^{\prime})\cdot c^{\operatorname{CF}}(V^{\prime\prime})
. This shows that the assignment
V\mapsto c^{\operatorname{CF}}(V)
extends to a group homomorphism

c^{\operatorname{CF}}(-):K_{0}(X)\to(1+\oplus_{J}H{\mathbb{Z}}^{2\langle J\rangle,\langle J\rangle}(X)t^{J})^{\times}.

In particular, for each partition
I
(for instance,
I=(n)
), we have a well-defined map (of sets)

c_{I}:K_{0}(X)\to H{\mathbb{Z}}^{2|I|,|I|}(X).

2.4.
Algebraic cobordism

The universal oriented cohomology theory is Voevodsky’s algebraic cobordism
{\operatorname{MGL}}
. Briefly, we can consider the spaces
{\operatorname{BGL}}_{r}:={\operatorname*{colim}}_{n}{\operatorname{Gr}}(r,n)\in{\operatorname{Spc}}(k)
with respect to the natural inclusions
{\operatorname{Gr}}(r,n)\hookrightarrow{\operatorname{Gr}}(r,n+1)
and
{\operatorname{MGL}}_{r}={\operatorname{Th}}(E_{r})\in{\operatorname{Spc}}_{\bullet}(k)
with
E_{r}\to{\operatorname{BGL}}_{r}
tautological bundle. There are natural inclusions
i_{r}:{\operatorname{BGL}}_{r}\hookrightarrow{\operatorname{BGL}}_{r+1}
, and isomorphisms
i_{r}^{*}E_{r=1}\simeq\mathcal{O}\oplus E_{r}
, which induce the bonding maps
\epsilon_{r}:{\operatorname{Th}}(\mathcal{O}\oplus E_{r})\simeq T\wedge{\operatorname{MGL}}_{r}\to{\operatorname{MGL}}_{r+1}
, with
T={\mathbb{A}}^{1}/({\mathbb{A}}^{1}\setminus\{0\})
.
{\operatorname{MGL}}
is then the
T
-spectrum
({\operatorname{MGL}}_{0},{\operatorname{MGL}}_{1},\ldots)
, ad since
T
is weakly equivalent to
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, the homotopy category of
T
-spectra is equivalent to
{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
.

It is easy to see that this is equivalent to define
{\operatorname{MGL}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
as the colimit
{\operatorname*{colim}}_{r}\Sigma^{-2r,-r}\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{\infty}{\operatorname{MGL}}_{r}
, with respect to the maps
\Sigma^{-2r-2,-r-1}\Sigma^{\infty}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}\epsilon_{r}
. In particular, we have natural maps
\alpha_{r}:\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{\infty}{\operatorname{MGL}}_{r}\to\Sigma^{2r,2}{\operatorname{MGL}}
.

Remark 2.41
.

It is shown in
[
29
, Section 2.1]
that
{\operatorname{MGL}}
has the structure of a highly structured motivic commutative ring spectrum.

Remark 2.42
.

{\operatorname{MGL}}
has a natural
{\operatorname{GL}}
-orientation where, for
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
affine,
V\to X
a rank
r
vector bundle, and
\eta_{V}:X\to{\operatorname{BGL}}_{r}
a classifying map of
V
, which is defined up to
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-homotopy, the
{\operatorname{MGL}}
-Thom class of
V
is given by

{\operatorname{th}}^{\operatorname{MGL}}(V):\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{\infty}{\operatorname{Th}}(V)\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{Th}}(\eta_{V})}\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{\infty}{\operatorname{MGL}}_{r}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{r}}\Sigma^{2r,r}{\operatorname{MGL}}.

A standard Jouanolou argument allows us to extend this construction to the case where
X
is not affine. All the axioms for a theory of Thom classes are straightforward to check.

The
{\operatorname{GL}}
-orientation on
{\operatorname{MGL}}
of Remark
2.42
is universal in the following sense:

Theorem 2.43

(
[
29
]
, Theorem 2.7)
.

For any commutative ring spectrum
{\mathcal{E}}
, the map of sets

\{\text{morphisms of commutative ring spectra}\;{\operatorname{MGL}}\to{\mathcal{E}}\;\text{in}\;\;{\operatorname{SH}}(k)\}\\
\to\{\text{orientations on the ring spectrum}\;{\mathcal{E}}\}

that takes a map
\varphi:{\operatorname{MGL}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
to the
{\operatorname{GL}}
-orientation of
{\mathcal{E}}
given by
{\operatorname{th}}^{\mathcal{E}}(V):=\Sigma^{2r,r}\varphi\circ{\operatorname{th}}^{\operatorname{MGL}}(V)
is a bijection.

The
{\operatorname{GL}}
-orientation on
{\operatorname{MGL}}
induces a formal group law on
{\operatorname{MGL}}^{*}
. This in turn gives a map
{\mathbb{L}\text{az}}\to{\operatorname{MGL}}
from the Lazard ring, which is the ring of coefficient of the universal formal group law
F^{\text{univ}}\in{\mathbb{L}\text{az}}[[x,y]]
. Spitzweck (
[
43
]
) and Hoyois (
[
16
]
) proved that, after inverting the exponential characteristic of
k
, this map is an isomorphism of graded ring, and that
{\operatorname{MGL}}^{2n+m,n}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)[1/p]=0
for all
m>0
. Also, it is well-known since Quillen that
{\mathbb{L}\text{az}}
is isomorphic to the polynomial ring
{\mathbb{Z}}[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots]
with
x_{i}
in degree
-i
for all
i>0
(see e.g.
[
21
, Theorem 4.4.9]
for a proof). In particular, we have the following.

Theorem 2.44
.

After inverting the exponential characteristic of
k
,
{\operatorname{MGL}}^{*}
is isomorphic to the polynomial ring
{\mathbb{Z}}[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots]
with
\mid x_{i}\mid=-i
.

Through a well-established algebraic version of the Pontryagin-Thom construction (see
[
22
, Section 3]
), we can associate to any smooth proper variety over
k
a class in the algebraic cobordism ring
{\operatorname{MGL}}^{*}
, as follows.

Definition 2.45
.

If
X
is a smooth proper variety
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
of dimension
d
, we call the
{\operatorname{MGL}}
-class of
X
the class
[X]_{\operatorname{MGL}}=[X,-T_{X},{\operatorname{id}}]_{\operatorname{MGL}}\in{\operatorname{MGL}}_{2d,d}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)={\operatorname{MGL}}^{-2d,-d}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)
constructed as in Definition
2.33
. Explicitly, if
p:X\to{\operatorname{Spec}}k
is the structure map,
[X]_{\operatorname{MGL}}
is given by the composition

1_{k}\xrightarrow{p^{\vee}}p_{\#}\Sigma^{-T_{X}}1_{X}\xrightarrow{p_{\#}\Sigma^{-T_{X}}\epsilon_{p^{*}{\operatorname{MGL}}}}p_{\#}\Sigma^{-T_{X}}p^{*}{\operatorname{MGL}}\xrightarrow{p_{\#}\Sigma^{-2d,-d}{\operatorname{th}}_{{\operatorname{MGL}}}^{f}(-T_{X})}\\
p_{\#}\Sigma^{-2d,-d}p^{*}{\operatorname{MGL}}\simeq p_{\#}p^{*}\Sigma^{-2d,-d}{\operatorname{MGL}}\xrightarrow{\eta_{(p_{\#},p^{*})}(\Sigma^{-2d,-d}{\operatorname{MGL}})}\Sigma^{-2d,-d}{\operatorname{MGL}}.

Remark 2.46
.

Taking
p_{*}:{\operatorname{MGL}}^{-2d,-d}(p_{X\#}\Sigma^{-T_{X}}1_{X})\cong{\operatorname{MGL}}^{0,0}(X)\to{\operatorname{MGL}}^{-2d,-d}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)
the proper pushforward,
[X]_{\operatorname{MGL}}
can be seen as the class
p_{*}{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{MGL}}}(-T_{X})
.

Proposition 2.47

(
[
22
]
, Theorem 3.4 (4))
.

After inverting the exponential characteristic of
k
,
{\operatorname{MGL}}_{2d,d}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)
is generated by the classes
[X]_{\operatorname{MGL}}
, for
X
smooth proper
k
-scheme with
{\operatorname{dim}}_{k}(X)=d
.

It is well known (see for example
[
29
, Theorem 2.2]
) that
H{\mathbb{Z}}^{*,*}({\operatorname{BGL}}_{r})=H{\mathbb{Z}}^{*,*}(k)[c_{1},\ldots,c_{r}],
with
c_{r}
in bidegree
(2r,r)
being the usual
r
-th Chern class
c_{r}(E_{m})
for
m\geq r
, defined by the Grothendieck formula (
2.12
). Moreover, the cohomology pullback
i_{r}^{*}:H{\mathbb{Z}}^{a,b}({\operatorname{BGL}}_{r+1})\to H{\mathbb{Z}}^{a,b}({\operatorname{BGL}}_{r})
is surjective, thus, the inverse system
\{H{\mathbb{Z}}^{a,b}({\operatorname{BGL}}_{r}),i_{r}^{*}\}_{r}
satisfies the Mittag-Leffler condition, and its
\lim^{1}
-term vanishes. Using this and the Thom isomorphism
H{\mathbb{Z}}^{a+2r,b+r}({\operatorname{MGL}}_{r})\cong H{\mathbb{Z}}^{a,b}({\operatorname{BGL}}_{r})
given by the
{\operatorname{GL}}
-orientation of
H{\mathbb{Z}}
, it is easy to prove the following.

Proposition 2.48
.

We have
H{\mathbb{Z}}^{*,*}({\operatorname{MGL}})\cong H{\mathbb{Z}}^{*,*}(k)[c_{1},c_{2},\ldots],

with
c_{r}
in bidegree
(2r,r)
. The same holds if we replace
H{\mathbb{Z}}
with
H{\mathbb{Z}}/\ell
for any prime
\ell
.

For each
r\geq 0
, we have the flag bundle
{\operatorname{Fl}}_{r}:={\operatorname{Fl}}(E_{r})\xrightarrow{\pi_{r}}{\operatorname{BGL}}_{r}
associated to the tautological bundle
E_{r}\to{\operatorname{BGL}}_{r}
. By construction of the flag bundles, the inclusions
i_{r}:{\operatorname{BGL}}_{r}\hookrightarrow{\operatorname{BGL}}_{r+1}
induce maps
{\operatorname{Fl}}(i_{r}):{\operatorname{Fl}}_{r}\to{\operatorname{Fl}}_{r+1}
, and we have

{\operatorname{Fl}}(i_{r})^{*}(\pi_{r+1}^{*}E_{r+1})\simeq\pi_{r}^{*}(i_{r}^{*}E_{r+1})\simeq\pi_{r}^{*}(E_{r}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{{\operatorname{BGL}}_{r}})\simeq L_{1}^{(r)}\oplus\ldots\oplus L_{r}^{(r)}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{{\operatorname{Fl}}_{r}}.

Since
\pi_{r+1}^{*}E_{r+1}\simeq L_{1}^{(r+1)}\oplus\ldots\oplus L_{r+1}^{(r+1)}
, we can suppose
{\operatorname{Fl}}(i_{r})^{*}(L_{j}^{(r+1)})=L_{j}^{(r)}
for all
j<r
, and then
{\operatorname{Fl}}(i_{r})^{*}\xi_{j}(E_{r+1})=\xi_{j}(E_{r})
for all
j<r
. We define the bundle
