{\operatorname{Fl}}\to{\operatorname{BGL}}
through
{\operatorname{Fl}}:={\operatorname*{colim}}_{r}({\operatorname{Fl}}_{0}\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{Fl}}(i_{0})}{\operatorname{Fl}}_{1}\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{Fl}}(i_{1})}\ldots)
, and the class
\xi_{j}(E_{r})
, for some
r>>0
, gives then a class
\xi_{j}\in{\operatorname{Fl}}
. The maps
\pi_{r}:{\operatorname{Fl}}_{r}\to{\operatorname{BGL}}_{r}
induce a map
{\operatorname{Fl}}\to{\operatorname{BGL}}
by passing to the colimit, and
H{\mathbb{Z}}^{*,*}({\operatorname{Fl}})\cong\lim_{r}H{\mathbb{Z}}^{*,*}({\operatorname{Fl}}_{r})
, thus, the map
\pi^{*}:H{\mathbb{Z}}^{2*,*}({\operatorname{BGL}})\to H{\mathbb{Z}}^{2*,*}({\operatorname{Fl}})
satisfies the splitting principle.

Definition 2.49
.

We call the
n
-th universal Newton class
the class
c_{(n)}\in H{\mathbb{Z}}^{2n,n}({\operatorname{MGL}})\cong H{\mathbb{Z}}^{2n,n}({\operatorname{BGL}})
corresponding to the symmetric function
\sum_{i}\xi_{i}^{n}\in H{\mathbb{Z}}^{2n,n}({\operatorname{Fl}})
as in Definition
2.38
(4).

Let
h:=h_{H{\mathbb{Z}}}:{\operatorname{MGL}}_{*,*}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)\to H{\mathbb{Z}}_{*,*}({\operatorname{MGL}})
be the motivic Hurewicz map as defined in Remark
2.5
. For any cohomological class
\alpha\in H\mathbb{Z}^{a,b}({\operatorname{MGL}})
and any homological class
\gamma\in H\mathbb{Z}_{c,d}({\operatorname{MGL}})
, we have the pairing
\langle\alpha,\gamma\rangle\in H\mathbb{Z}^{a-c,b-d}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)
, as in (
2.1
) with
{\mathcal{E}}=H{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Definition 2.50
.

For
x\in{\operatorname{MGL}}^{-n}={\operatorname{MGL}}_{2n,n}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)
, and
c_{(n)}\in H{\mathbb{Z}}^{2n,n}({\operatorname{MGL}})
the
n
-th universal Newton class (Definition
2.49
), we denote by
c_{(n)}(x)
the integer

c_{(n)}(x):=\langle c_{(n)},h(x)\rangle\in H{\mathbb{Z}}^{0,0}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)={\mathbb{Z}}.

If
\ell
is an odd prime different from
{\operatorname{char}}k
,
[
22
]
gives the following generating criterion for the
\ell
-adic completion
({\operatorname{MGL}}^{*})^{\wedge}_{\ell}
.

Proposition 2.51

(
[
22
]
, from proof of Theorem B, Section 6.6)
.

A family of elements
\{x_{d}^{\prime}\}_{d\geq 1}
, with
x_{d}^{\prime}\in({\operatorname{MGL}}^{-d})^{\wedge}_{\ell}
forms a family of polynomial generators of
({\operatorname{MGL}}^{*})^{\wedge}_{\ell}
if and only if

\nu_{\ell}(c_{(d)}(x_{d}^{\prime}))=\begin{cases}1\;\;\;\;\text{for}\;\;d=\ell^{r}-1,\;r\geq 1\\
0\;\;\;\text{for}\;\;d\neq\ell^{r}-1\;\forall r\geq 1.\end{cases}

2.5.
{\operatorname{Sp}}
-oriented theories

We recall that a rank
2r

symplectic vector bundle
over
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
is a pair
(V,\omega)
, where
V\to X
is a rank
2r
vector bundle, and
\omega:V\otimes V\to\mathcal{O}_{X}
is a symplectic form on it, namely a non-degenerate antisymmetric bilinear form.

Example 2.52
.

(1)

The trivial rank
2r
vector bundle
\mathcal{O}_{X}^{2r}
can be equipped with the standard rank
2r
symplectic form
\phi_{2r}
, represented by the standard
2r\times 2r
symplectic matrix

\phi_{2r}=\begin{pmatrix}&&&&&1\\
&&&&\ldots&\\
&&&1&&\\
&&-1&&&\\
&\ldots&&&&\\
-1&&&&&\end{pmatrix}.

(2)

For
V\to X
any rank
r
vector bundle, the rank
2r
vector bundle
V\oplus V^{\vee}\to X
can always be equipped with the rank
2r
standard symplectic form
\phi_{2r}
. Concretely, this is the form

\phi_{2r}((v,v^{*}),(w,w^{*}))\coloneqq<w^{*},v>-<v^{*},w>,

where
<-,->:V^{\vee}\times V\to\mathcal{O}_{X}
is the canonical pairing.

The axioms defining a
{\operatorname{GL}}
-orientation as in Definition
2.23
can be used to define weaker kinds of orientation, namely a theory of Thom classes for vector bundles carrying additional structures, as elaborated by Panin and Walter in
[
31
]
and
[
33
]
. We are interested in the following.

Definition 2.53
.

An
{\operatorname{Sp}}
-orientation for a commutative ring spectrum
{\mathcal{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
consists in a theory of Thom classes
{\operatorname{th}}_{{\operatorname{Sp}}}^{\mathcal{E}}(V,\omega)\in{\mathcal{E}}^{4r,2r}({\operatorname{Th}}(V))
for each rank
2r
symplectc vector bundle
(V,\omega)\to X
,
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
, satisfying the axioms of Definition
2.23
, after replacing the trivial vector bundle
\mathcal{O}^{r}_{X}
with the trivial symplectic bundle
(\mathcal{O}_{X}^{2r},\phi_{2r})
, morphisms and exact sequences of vector bundles with morphisms and exact sequences of symplectic vector bundles.

It is immediate to note that any
{\operatorname{GL}}
-orientation induces an
{\operatorname{Sp}}
-orientation.

We now want to get a theory of symplectic Thom isomorphisms as in the case of
{\operatorname{GL}}
-oriented theories. We first need to define symplectic analogs of the fundamental groupoid
{\mathcal{K}}(X)
and the Grothendieck group
K_{0}(X)
.

Definition 2.54
.

Let us fix a scheme
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
.

1. Let
{\mathcal{S}}{p}(X)
denote the groupoid whose objects are the symplectic vector bundles
(V,\omega)
on
X
, and whose morphisms are isomorphisms of vector bundles compatible with the given symplectic forms.

2. Let
{\mathcal{S}}{p}(X)_{st}
be the category where objects are pairs of symplectic vector bundles on
X

((V,\omega),(V^{\prime},\omega^{\prime}))
, and where a morphism
((V_{1},\omega_{1}),(V_{1}^{\prime},\omega_{1}^{\prime}))\to((V_{2},\omega_{2}),(V_{2}^{\prime},\omega_{2}^{\prime}))
is a triple
((V,\omega),f,f^{\prime})
with
(V,\omega)
a
symplectic vector bundle on
X
, and

f:(V_{1}\oplus V,\omega_{1}\oplus\omega)\xrightarrow{\sim}(V_{2},\omega_{2}),\;\;f^{\prime}:(V^{\prime}_{1}\oplus V,\omega^{\prime}_{1}\oplus\omega)\xrightarrow{\sim}(V^{\prime}_{2},\omega^{\prime}_{2})

are isomorphisms of symplectic vector bundles. If we have two morphisms

((V_{1},\omega_{1}),(V_{1}^{\prime},\omega_{1}^{\prime}))\xrightarrow{((V_{f},\omega_{f}),f,f^{\prime})}((V_{2},\omega_{2}),(V_{2}^{\prime},\omega_{2}^{\prime}))\xrightarrow{((V_{g},\omega_{g}),g,g^{\prime})}((V_{3},\omega_{3}),(V_{3}^{\prime},\omega_{3}^{\prime})),

their composition is the triple
((V_{f}\oplus V_{g},\omega_{f}\oplus\omega_{g}),g\circ(f\oplus{\operatorname{id}}_{V_{g}}),g^{\prime}\circ(f^{\prime}\oplus{\operatorname{id}}_{V_{g}}))
.

3. Let
{\mathcal{K}}^{\operatorname{Sp}}(X)
be the localization of
{\mathcal{S}}{p}(X)_{st}
with respect to the morphisms

((V^{\prime\prime},\omega^{\prime\prime}),{\operatorname{id}}_{V\oplus V^{\prime\prime}},{\operatorname{id}}_{V^{\prime}\oplus V^{\prime\prime}}):((V,\omega),(V^{\prime},\omega^{\prime}))\to((V\oplus V^{\prime\prime},\omega\oplus\omega^{\prime\prime}),(V^{\prime}\oplus V^{\prime\prime},\omega^{\prime}\oplus\omega^{\prime\prime})).

4. We have the commutative monoid
({\mathcal{S}}{p}(X)/\text{iso},\oplus)
of isomorphism classes in
{\mathcal{S}}{p}(X)
, with operation induced by the direct sum of symplectic bundles. Let
K_{0}^{\operatorname{Sp}}(X)
denote its group completion.

Remark 2.55
.

1.
{\mathcal{K}}^{\operatorname{Sp}}(X)
is a symmetric monoidal groupoid: the inverse of
((V,\omega),f,f^{\prime}):((V_{1},\omega_{1}),(V_{1}^{\prime},\omega_{1}^{\prime}))\to((V_{2},\omega_{2}),(V_{2}^{\prime},\omega_{2}^{\prime}))
is
((V,\omega),{\operatorname{id}}_{V_{1}\oplus V},{\operatorname{id}}_{V_{1}^{\prime}\oplus V})^{-1}\circ(0,f^{-1},f^{\prime-1})
, and the symmetric monoidal product is induced by direct sum.

2. Sending
((V_{1},\omega_{1}),(V_{1}^{\prime},\omega_{1}^{\prime}))
to the formal difference of classes
[(V_{1},\omega_{1})]-[(V_{1}^{\prime},\omega_{1}^{\prime})]\in K_{0}^{\operatorname{Sp}}(X)
descends to an isomorphism of abelian groups
{\mathcal{K}}^{\operatorname{Sp}}(X)/\text{iso}\cong K_{0}^{\operatorname{Sp}}(X)
.

3. Sending
((V_{1},\omega_{1}),(V_{1}^{\prime},\omega_{1}^{\prime}))
to the complex
V_{1}\oplus V_{1}^{\prime}[1]\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)
extends to a functor of symmetric monoidal groupoids
{\mathcal{K}}^{\operatorname{Sp}}(X)\to{\mathcal{K}}(X)
, which induces the corresponding map of abelian groups
K_{0}^{\operatorname{Sp}}(X)\to K_{0}(X)
by passing to isomorphism classes. In particular, sending
((V_{1},\omega_{1}),(V_{1}^{\prime},\omega_{1}^{\prime}))
to
\Sigma^{V_{1}-V_{1}^{\prime}}1_{X}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(X)
extends to a functor of groupoids
\Sigma^{(-)}:{\mathcal{K}}^{\operatorname{Sp}}(X)\to{\operatorname{SH}}(X)_{\text{iso}}
.

Definition 2.56
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
be an
{\operatorname{Sp}}
-oriented ring spectrum. Given a rank
2r
symplectic bundle,
(V,\omega)
on
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
, the Thom class
{\operatorname{th}}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{Sp}}(V,\omega)
induces a morphism

{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}}}_{\mathcal{E}}(V,\omega):\Sigma^{V-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{2r}}1_{X}\to p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}

in
{\operatorname{SH}}(X)
, where
p_{X}:X\to{\operatorname{Spec}}k
is the structure map, which in turn induces the morphism

{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}},f}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(V,\omega):\Sigma^{V-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{2r}}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\to p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}

in
{\operatorname{SH}}(X)
. If
{\mathcal{E}}
is highly structured,
{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}}}_{\mathcal{E}}(V,\omega)
also induces the map

{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}},f}_{{\mathcal{E}}{\operatorname{Mod}}}(V,\omega):\Sigma^{V-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{2r}}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\to p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}

in
{\operatorname{Mod}}_{p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}}
, by the free-forget adjunction.

Lemma 2.57
.

The map
{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}},f}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(V,\omega)
is an isomorphism in
{\operatorname{SH}}(X)
, and if
{\mathcal{E}}
is highly structured, the map
{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}},f}_{{\mathcal{E}}{\operatorname{Mod}}}(V,\omega)
is an isomorphism in
{\operatorname{Mod}}_{p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}}
.

Proof.

The statement for
{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}},f}_{{\mathcal{E}}{\operatorname{Mod}}}(V,\omega)
follows immediately from that for
{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}},f}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(V,\omega)
, by applying the forgetful functor. We prove the statement for
{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}},f}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(V,\omega)
.

Let us suppose that
(V,\omega)
is a trivial symplectic vector bundle, which means
V=\mathcal{O}_{X}^{2r}
,
\omega=\phi_{2r}
. Then we have
\Sigma^{V}=\Sigma^{4r,2r}
, and it is easy to see that
{\operatorname{th}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\operatorname{Sp}}(V,\omega)
can be rewritten as

{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}},f}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(V,\omega)=\Sigma^{4r,2r}\mu_{p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}}\circ\Sigma^{4r,2r}(\epsilon_{p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}}\wedge 1)=\Sigma^{4r,2r}{\operatorname{id}}_{p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}},

and hence is an isomorphism.

Let then
(V,\omega)
be a general rank
2r
symplectic vector bundle over
X
. If
x\in X
is a point, through a skew-symmetric version of the Gram-Schmidt process, we can find (not-uniquely) a Zariski open neighbourhood
U_{x}
of
x
and a decomposition

V\mid_{U_{x}}=\langle v_{1},\ldots,v_{2r}\rangle

such that
\omega\mid_{U_{x}}
is represented by the standard symplectic matrix
\phi_{2r}
with respect to the generating sections
v_{1},\ldots v_{2r}
. We have then a Zariski open cover
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha}
trivializing
(V,\omega)
as symplectic vector bundle. By quasi-compactness we can suppose
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{\alpha_{1}},\ldots,U_{\alpha_{n}}\}
finite.

To simplify the notation, we write
\Psi
for
{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}},f}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(V,\omega)
. Let
X_{i}=\cup_{j=1}^{i}U_{\alpha_{i}}
with open immersion
j_{i}:X_{i}\to X
. We show by induction on
i
that
j_{i}^{*}\Psi:j_{i}^{*}\Sigma^{V}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\to j_{i}^{*}\Sigma^{4r,2r}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}
is an isomorphism, the case
i=1
following by our construction of
U_{\alpha_{1}}
.

Let us take
i>1
and write
X_{i}=X_{i-1}\cup U_{\alpha_{i}}
. Let
j:X_{i-1}\cap U_{\alpha_{i}}\hookrightarrow X_{i}
,
j_{1}:X_{i-1}\hookrightarrow X_{i}
and
j_{2}:U_{\alpha_{i}}\hookrightarrow X_{i}
be the evident open immersions. Lemma
2.58
below shows that there exists a Mayer-Vietoris distinguished triangle of endofunctors of
{\operatorname{SH}}(X_{i})
:

(2.13)

j_{\#}j^{*}\to j_{1\#}j_{1}^{*}\oplus j_{2\#}j_{2}^{*}\to{\operatorname{id}}_{{\operatorname{SH}}(X_{i})}\to j_{\#}j^{*}[1].

Applying (
2.13
) to the map of spectra
j_{i}^{*}\Psi
gives the commutative diagram in
{\operatorname{SH}}(X_{i})

\textstyle{j_{\#}j^{*}j_{i}^{*}\Sigma^{V}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{\#}j^{*}j_{i}^{*}\Psi}
\begin{matrix}j_{1\#}j_{i-1}^{*}\Psi\\
\oplus\\
j_{2\#}j_{2}^{*}j_{i}^{*}\Psi\end{matrix}
\scriptstyle{j_{1\#}j_{i-1}^{*}\Psi\oplus j_{2\#}j_{2}^{*}j_{i}^{*}\Psi}
\textstyle{j_{i}^{*}\Sigma^{V}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{i}^{*}\Psi}
\textstyle{j_{\#}j^{*}j_{i}^{*}\Sigma^{V}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{\#}j^{*}j_{i}^{*}\Psi[1]}
\textstyle{j_{\#}j^{*}j_{i}^{*}\Sigma^{(4r,2r)}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\begin{matrix}j_{1\#}j_{i-1}^{*}\Sigma^{(4r,2r)}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\\
\oplus\\
j_{2\#}j_{2}^{*}j_{i}^{*}\Sigma^{(4r,2r)}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\end{matrix}
\textstyle{j_{i}^{*}\Sigma^{(4r,2r)}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{j_{\#}j^{*}j_{i}^{*}\Sigma^{(4r,2r)}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}[1],}

with rows distinguished triangles. Using the induction hypothesis, we see that the vertical maps in the first, second, and fourth columns are isomorphisms, hence

j_{i}^{*}\Psi
is an isomorphism and the induction goes through.
∎

Lemma 2.58
.

Let
X=U_{1}\cup U_{2}
be the union of two Zariski open subsets
U_{1},U_{2}
, and let
j_{1}:U_{1}\to X
,
j_{2}:U_{2}\to X
and
j:U_{1}\cap U_{2}\to X
the respective open inclusions. Then we have a Mayer-Vietoris distinguished triangle

j_{\#}j^{*}\to j_{1\#}j_{1}^{*}\oplus j_{2\#}j_{2}^{*}\to{\operatorname{id}}_{{\operatorname{SH}}(X)}\to j_{\#}j^{*}\to j_{1\#}j_{1}^{*}[1]

of endofunctors of
{\operatorname{SH}}(X)
.

Proof.

Let us consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{1}\cap U_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{1}\cup U_{2}=X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{1}}

Let
i_{1}:X-U_{1}\to X
be the closed complement of
j_{1}
, and smilarly, let
i_{2}
be the closed complement of
t_{2}
. From Proposition
2.14
, the localization sequence for the pair
(j_{1},i_{1})
is the distinguished triangle

(2.14)

j_{1!}j_{1}^{*}\to{\operatorname{id}}_{{\operatorname{SH}}(X)}\to i_{1*}i_{1}^{*}\to j_{1!}j_{1}^{*}[1]

of endofunctors of
{\operatorname{SH}}(X)
. Analogously, the localization sequence for the pair
(t_{2},i_{2})
is the distinguished triangle

t_{2!}t_{2}^{*}\to{\operatorname{id}}_{{\operatorname{SH}}(X)}\to i_{2*}i_{2}^{*}\to t_{2!}t_{2}^{*}[1].

By applying
j_{2!}(-)j_{2}^{*}
to the last triangle, and using
j_{2}\circ t_{2}=j
, we get the distinguished triangle

(2.15)

j_{!}j^{*}\to j_{2!}j_{2}^{*}\to j_{2!}i_{2*}i_{2}^{*}j_{2}^{*}\to j_{!}j^{*}[1].

Moreover, we have a canonical distinguished triangle

(2.16)

j_{1!}j_{1}^{*}\to j_{1!}j_{1}^{*}\oplus j_{2!}j_{2}^{*}\to j_{2!}j_{2}^{*}\to j_{1!}j_{1}^{*}[1].

Since
i_{1}\simeq j_{2}\circ i_{2}
on
U_{2}-(U_{1}\cap U_{2})
, we have that
j_{2!}i_{2*}i_{2}^{*}j_{2}^{*}\simeq i_{1*}i_{1}^{*}
. Thus, we can apply the octahedral axiom to the triangles (
2.14
), (
2.15
) and (
2.16
), and we get the distinguished triangle

j_{!}j^{*}\to j_{1!}j_{1}^{*}\oplus j_{2!}j_{2}^{*}\to{\operatorname{id}}_{{\operatorname{SH}}(X)}\to j_{!}j^{*}\to j_{1!}j_{1}^{*}[1].

Finally, for any
f
open immersion, we have
f_{!}\simeq f_{\#}
by Remark
2.21
. This concludes the proof.
∎

We have proved that
{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}},f}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(V,\omega)
is an isomorphism. We may then define the map of spectra

{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}},f}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(-(V,\omega)):\Sigma^{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{2r}-V}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\to p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}

by
{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}},f}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(-(V,\omega)):=\Sigma^{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{2r}-V}({\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}},f}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(V,\omega)^{-1})
, and then define

{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(-(V,\omega)):\Sigma^{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{2r}-V}1_{X}\to p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}

by composing
{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}},f}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(-(V,\omega))
with the map
\Sigma^{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{2r}-V}1_{X}\to\Sigma^{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{2r}-V}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}
induced by the unit
\epsilon_{\mathcal{E}}
of
{\mathcal{E}}
. Finally, if
(V^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
is another symplectic vector bundle of rank
2r^{\prime}
, we define

{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}((V,\omega)-(V^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})):\Sigma^{V-V^{\prime}-({\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{2r}-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{2r^{\prime}})}1_{X}\to p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}

as the composition

\Sigma^{V-V^{\prime}-({\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{2r}-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{2r^{\prime}})}1_{X}\simeq\Sigma^{V-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{2r}}(\Sigma^{-V^{\prime}+{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{2r^{\prime}}}1_{X})\\
\xrightarrow{\Sigma^{V-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{2r}}({\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(-(V^{\prime},\omega^{\prime}))}\Sigma^{V-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{2r}}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}},f}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(V,\omega)}p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}.

Again, we also have the
p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}
-extension

{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}},f}_{{\mathcal{E}}}((V,\omega)-(V^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})):\Sigma^{V-V^{\prime}-({\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{2r}-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{2r^{\prime}})}p^{*}_{X}{\mathcal{E}}\to p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}},

given by

{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}},f}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(V,\omega)\circ\Sigma^{V-V^{\prime}-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{2r}-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{2r^{\prime}}}({\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}},f}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(V^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})^{-1})
, and similarly, if
{\mathcal{E}}
is highly structured, the map
{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}},f}_{{\mathcal{E}}{\operatorname{Mod}}}((V,\omega)-(V^{\prime},\omega^{\prime}))
of
p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}
-modules.

The multiplicativity of the symplectic Thom isomorphisms, as stated in Lemma
2.59
below, follows as for the
{\operatorname{GL}}
-oriented case from the multiplicativity of the symplectic Thom classes, following the symplectic version of (
2.8
)(3).

Lemma 2.59
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
be symplectically oriented, and take
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
.
For
v,v^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{K}}^{\operatorname{Sp}}(X)
,
{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}^{\operatorname{Sp}}(v+v^{\prime})
is the composition

(2.17)

\Sigma^{v+v^{\prime}-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(v+v^{\prime})}}1_{X}\simeq\Sigma^{v-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(v)}}1_{X}\wedge_{X}\Sigma^{v^{\prime}-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(v^{\prime})}}1_{X}\\
\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}^{\operatorname{Sp}}(v)\wedge{\operatorname{th}}^{\operatorname{Sp}}_{\mathcal{E}}(v^{\prime})}p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\wedge_{X}p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\xrightarrow{\mu_{p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}}}p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}.

Proposition 2.60
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
be a symplectically oriented ring spectrum and let
p_{X}:X\to{\operatorname{Spec}}k
be in
{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
.

1. Let
c_{p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}}:{\mathcal{K}}^{\operatorname{Sp}}(X)\to{\operatorname{SH}}(X)_{{\operatorname{id}}}
be the constant functor with value
p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}
, with
{\operatorname{SH}}(X)_{{\operatorname{id}}}
considered as a category with only identity morphisms. Then sending
(V,\omega)-(V^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
to
{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}((V,\omega)-(V^{\prime},\omega^{\prime}))
extends to a natural transformation of functors

({\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(-):\Sigma^{(-)-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(-)}}1_{X}\to c_{p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}}):{\mathcal{K}}^{\operatorname{Sp}}(X)\to{\operatorname{SH}}(X).

2. The functor of groupoids
\Sigma^{(-)-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(-)}}p^{*}_{X}{\mathcal{E}}:{\mathcal{K}}^{\operatorname{Sp}}(X)\to({\operatorname{SH}}(X))_{\simeq}
descends (up to equivalence) to a functor
\Sigma^{(-)-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(-)}}p^{*}_{X}{\mathcal{E}}:K_{0}^{\operatorname{Sp}}(X)\to{\operatorname{SH}}(X)
, where we consider

K_{0}^{\operatorname{Sp}}(X)
as a category with only identity morphisms.

3. For each
(v,\omega)\in K^{\operatorname{Sp}}_{0}(X)
, let
{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}},f}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(v,\omega):\Sigma^{v-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(v)}}p^{*}_{X}{\mathcal{E}}\to p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}
be the
{\mathcal{E}}
-linear extension of

{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}},f}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(v,\omega)
. Then the maps
{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}},f}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(v,\omega)
define a natural isomorphism of functors of groupoids

{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}},f}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(-):\Sigma^{(-)-{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{{\operatorname{rank}}(-)}}p^{*}_{X}{\mathcal{E}}\to c_{p_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}}:K_{0}^{\operatorname{Sp}}(X)\to({\operatorname{SH}}(X))_{\simeq}.

The proof is similar to that of Proposition
2.28
for the
{\operatorname{GL}}
-oriented case, but using Lemma
2.59
to show that the maps
{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(-)
are natural in
{\mathcal{K}}^{\operatorname{Sp}}(X)
. We omit the details.

Remark 2.61
.

For
{\mathcal{E}}
highly structured, we may refine Proposition
2.60
by replacing
{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}},f}_{{\mathcal{E}}}
with
{\operatorname{th}}^{{\operatorname{Sp}},f}_{{\mathcal{E}}{\operatorname{Mod}}}
and the target category
{\operatorname{SH}}(X)
with
{\operatorname{Mod}}_{\mathcal{E}}
.

Borel classes

The symplectic analogue of a theory of Chern classes is a theory of Borel classes. For
{\mathcal{E}}

{\operatorname{Sp}}
-oriented,
(V,\omega)\to X
rank
2r
symplectic vector bundle, one has Borel classes
b_{i}(V,\omega)\in{\mathcal{E}}^{4i,2i}(X)
,
i=1,\ldots,r
satisfying the axioms of
[
33
, Definition 14.1]
.

Alternatively, one can define the first Borel class of a rank
2
symplectic vector bundle
(V,\omega)\to X
as
-z_{0}^{*}{\operatorname{th}}_{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\mathcal{E}}(V,\omega)
, with
z_{0}:X_{+}\to{\operatorname{Th}}(V)
the zero section. Next, to any symplectic vector bundle
(V,\omega)\to X
, one has the associated quaternionic projective bundle
{\operatorname{HP}}(V,\omega)\to X
defined by
{\operatorname{HP}}(V,\omega):={\operatorname{Gr}}(2,V)/{\operatorname{Gr}}{\operatorname{Sp}}(2,V,\omega)
, where
{\operatorname{Gr}}{\operatorname{Sp}}(2,V,\omega)
is the closed subscheme of
{\operatorname{Gr}}(2,V)
parametrizing rank
2
vector subbundles of
V
over which the restriction of
\omega
is degenerate. The following is
[
33
, Theorem 8.2]
.

Theorem 2.62

(Quaternionic projective bundle formula)
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
be an
{\operatorname{Sp}}
-oriented commutative ring spectrum. Let
(V,\omega)\to X
be a rank
2r
symplectic vector bundle over
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
, and
{\operatorname{HP}}(V,\omega)\xrightarrow{q}X
the associated quaternionic projective bundle. Let
(\mathcal{U},\omega\mid_{\mathcal{U}})\to{\operatorname{HP}}(V,\omega)
be the tautological rank
2
symplectic vector bundle over
{\operatorname{HP}}(V,\omega)
, and
\xi\coloneqq b_{1}(\mathcal{U},\omega\mid_{\mathcal{U}})\in{\mathcal{E}}^{4,2}({\operatorname{HP}}(V,\omega))
its first Borel class. Then
{\mathcal{E}}^{*,*}({\operatorname{HP}}(V,\omega))
is a free
{\mathcal{E}}^{*,*}(X)
-module via the pullback
q^{*}
, with basis
1,\xi,\ldots,\xi^{r}
. Also, for
1\leq i\leq r
, there exist unique elements
b_{i}(V,\omega)\in{\mathcal{E}}^{4i,2i}(X)

such that

\xi^{r}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}(-1)^{i}b_{i}(V,\omega)\cup\xi^{r-i}=0,

and if
(V,\omega)
is the trivial, then
b_{i}(V,\omega)=0
for all
1\leq i\leq r
.

If
r=1
, one deduces from the discussion on symplectic Thom isomorphisms that the element
b_{1}(V,\omega)
given by Theorem
2.62
coincides with the previous description of the first Borel class of a rank
2
symplectic bundle, and one can define the Borel classes as the elements
b_{i}(V,\omega)
given by Theorem
2.62
. This approach is equivalent to the axiomatic one by
[
33
, Theorem 14.4]
.

Remark 2.63
.

The sign in the definition of the first Borel class is purely conventional, and it is chosen in order to satisfy the traditional relation
b_{i}(V)=(-1)^{i}c_{2i}
for cohomology theories that are both
{\operatorname{Sp}}
-oriented and
{\operatorname{GL}}
-oriented.

2.6.
Symplectic cobordism

A symplectic analogue of the algebraic cobordism spectrum
{\operatorname{MGL}}
is the symplectic cobordism spectrum
{\operatorname{MSp}}
defined in
[
30
, Section 6]
. The construction is similar to that of
{\operatorname{MGL}}
. If we equip
{\mathbb{A}}_{k}^{2n}
with the standard symplectic form
\phi_{2n}
, we can define the
quaternionic Grassmannian

{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)
as the open subscheme
j:{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)\xhookrightarrow{j}{\operatorname{Gr}}(2r,2n)
parametrizing the
2r
-dimensional subspaces of
{\mathbb{A}}_{k}^{2n}
on which the restriction of
\phi_{2n}
is non-degenerate. We denote by
E_{2r,2n}^{\operatorname{Sp}}
the restriction
j^{*}E_{2r,2n}
. In particular,
E_{2r,2n}^{\operatorname{Sp}}
is a subbundle of the trivial bundle
\mathcal{O}_{{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)}^{2n}
.
\phi_{2n}
defines a symplectic form on
\mathcal{O}_{{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)}^{2n}
, and its restriction to
E_{2r,2n}^{\operatorname{Sp}}
is non-degenerate. We call
(E_{2r,2n}^{\operatorname{Sp}},\phi_{2n}\mid_{E_{2r,2n}^{\operatorname{Sp}}})
the
tautological rank
2r
symplectic subbundle
. We will occasionally omit the symplectic form in the notation. One defines
{\operatorname{BSp}}_{2r}\in{\mathcal{H}}(k)
as the colimit
{\operatorname*{colim}}_{N}{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,rN)
with respect to the natural inclusions, the vector bundle
E_{2r}^{\operatorname{Sp}}\to{\operatorname{BSp}}_{2r}
as
E_{2r}^{\operatorname{Sp}}\coloneqq{\operatorname*{colim}}_{N}E_{2r,2rN}^{\operatorname{Sp}}
, and finally
{\operatorname{MSp}}_{2r}\coloneqq{\operatorname{Th}}(E_{2r}^{\operatorname{Sp}})\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\bullet}(k).
The canonical inclusion
i_{r}:{\operatorname{BSp}}_{2r}\to{\operatorname{BSp}}_{2r+2}
gives a canonical isomorphism
i^{*}E_{2r+2}^{\operatorname{Sp}}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{{\operatorname{BSp}}_{2r}}\oplus E_{2r}^{\operatorname{Sp}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{{\operatorname{BSp}}_{2r}}
. Analogously to the case of
{\operatorname{MGL}}
we get a
T^{\wedge 2}
-spectrum
{\operatorname{MGL}}=({\operatorname{MGL}}_{0},{\operatorname{MGL}}_{2},\ldots,{\operatorname{MGL}}_{2r},\ldots)
, and this defines an object in
{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
because the homotopy category of
T^{\wedge 2}
-spectra is equivalent to
{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
(
[
30
, Theorem 3.2]
). Of course, this is equivalent to defining
{\operatorname{MSp}}
as
{\operatorname*{colim}}_{r}\Sigma^{-4r,-2r}{\operatorname{MSp}}_{2r}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
.

Remark 2.64
.

It is shown in
[
30
, Section 6]
that
{\operatorname{MSp}}
lifts to a commutative monoid in the category of symmetric
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
-spectra. Thus,
{\operatorname{MSp}}
is a highly structured commutative ring spectrum in
{\operatorname{SH}}(k)

{\operatorname{MSp}}
comes with a theory of symplectic Thom classes, whose construction is completely analogous to that of
{\operatorname{MGL}}
, using
{\operatorname{BSp}}_{2r}
as classifying space for rank
2r
symplectic vector bundles, and we have the following universality theorem.

Theorem 2.65

(
[
32
]
, Theorem 4.5)
.

For any commutative ring spectrum
{\mathcal{E}}
, the map of sets

\{\text{morphisms of commutative ring spectra}\;{\operatorname{MSp}}\to{\mathcal{E}}\;\text{in}\;\;{\operatorname{SH}}(k)\}\\
\xrightarrow{\sim}\{\text{${\operatorname{Sp}}$-orientations on the ring spectrum}\;{\mathcal{E}}\}.

that takes a map
\varphi:{\mathcal{E}}\to{\operatorname{MSp}}
to the
{\operatorname{Sp}}
-orientation of
{\mathcal{E}}
given by
{\operatorname{th}}_{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\mathcal{E}}(V,\omega)\coloneqq\Sigma^{4r,2r}\varphi\circ{\operatorname{th}}_{\operatorname{Sp}}^{\operatorname{MSp}}(V,\omega)
is a bijection.

Remark 2.66
.

The inclusions
{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)\hookrightarrow{\operatorname{Gr}}(2r,2n)
induce maps
{\operatorname{BSp}}_{2r}\to{\operatorname{BGL}}_{2r}
, and, since
E_{2r}^{\operatorname{Sp}}
is the pullback of
E_{2r}
, we also get maps of Thom spaces
{\operatorname{MGL}}_{2r}\to{\operatorname{MSp}}_{2r}
in
{\mathcal{H}}_{\bullet}(k)
. These maps in turn define a natural map
\Phi:{\operatorname{MSp}}\to{\operatorname{MGL}}
in
{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
, and one notes that the
{\operatorname{Sp}}
-orientation on
{\operatorname{MGL}}
given by
\Phi
is the
{\operatorname{Sp}}
-orientation induced by the universal
{\operatorname{GL}}
-orientation on
{\operatorname{MGL}}
.

Proposition 2.67

(
[
30
]
, Theorems 9.1, 9.3)
.

Let
E^{\operatorname{Sp}}_{2r}\to{\operatorname{BSp}}_{2r}
be the tautological rank
2r
symplectic bundle, with Thom space
{\operatorname{MSp}}_{2r}
, and let
{\mathcal{E}}
be a
{\operatorname{Sp}}
-oriented commutative ring spectrum in
{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
.

1.
{\mathcal{E}}^{*,*}({\operatorname{BSp}}_{2r})
is the ring of homogeneous elements in the power series ring
{\mathcal{E}}^{*,*}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)[[b_{1},\ldots,b_{r}]]_{h}
, where
b_{i}:=b_{i}(E^{\operatorname{Sp}}_{2r})\in{\mathcal{E}}^{4i,2i}({\operatorname{BSp}}_{2r})
is the
i
-th Borel class of
E^{\operatorname{Sp}}_{2r}
.

2. Let
z_{2r}:{\operatorname{BSp}}_{2r}\to{\operatorname{MSp}}_{2r}
be the map induced by the zero-section of
E^{\operatorname{Sp}}_{2r}
. Then
z_{2r}^{*}:{\mathcal{E}}^{*,*}({\operatorname{MSp}}_{2r})\to{\mathcal{E}}^{*,*}({\operatorname{BSp}}_{2r})
is injective, with image the ideal generated by
b_{r}
.

From this, similarly to how we have shown in Proposition
2.48
for the motivic cohomology of
{\operatorname{MGL}}
, they obtain a computation of the
{\mathcal{E}}^{*,*}
-cohomology of
{\operatorname{MSp}}
, as follows.

Theorem 2.68

(
[
30
]
, Theorem 13.1)
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
any motivic commutative ring spectrum with an
{\operatorname{Sp}}
-orientation. Then we have an isomorphism of bigraded rings:

{\mathcal{E}}^{*,*}({\operatorname{MSp}})\cong{\mathcal{E}}^{*,*}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)[[b_{1},b_{2},\ldots]]_{h}

with the variables
b_{i}
in bidegree
(4i,2i)
.

Remark 2.69
.

If there is a
k_{0}
such that
{\mathcal{E}}^{a,b}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)=0
for
a<-4k_{0}
,
b<-2k_{0}
, then
{\mathcal{E}}^{*,*}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)[[b_{1},\ldots,b_{r}]]_{h}
is just the polynomial ring
{\mathcal{E}}^{*,*}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)[b_{1},\ldots,b_{r}]
. For instance, this is the case for
{\mathcal{E}}=H{\mathbb{Z}}
or
{\mathcal{E}}=H{\mathbb{Z}}/\ell
.

We do not have as nice description of the coefficient ring of
{\operatorname{MSp}}
as we have for
{\operatorname{MGL}}
through Theorem
2.44
and Propositions
2.47
,
2.51
. The main goal of this paper is a partial computation of the graded ring
{\operatorname{MSp}}^{*}
.

3.
A twisted degree map

For any
E
vector bundle on
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/S
and any
L
line bundle on
X
, Ananyevskiy (
[
2
, Lemma 4.1]
) gives an isomorphism in
{\mathcal{H}}_{\bullet}(S)
.

\rho:{\operatorname{Th}}(E\oplus L)\xrightarrow{\sim}{\operatorname{Th}}(E\oplus L^{\vee})

Through
\Sigma^{\infty}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}
, the isomorphism passes to the stable setting. Then, for line bundles
L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}
over
X
, we denote by
{\operatorname{Anan}}_{L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}}
the isomorphism

(3.1)

{\operatorname{Anan}}_{L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}}:\Sigma^{\oplus_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}}1_{X}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Sigma^{\oplus_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}^{\vee}}1_{X}

in
{\operatorname{SH}}(X)
obtained through Ananyevskiy’s construction, with
S=X
. In the same way, for all
e\in K_{0}(X)
and line bundles
L_{i}
as before, we obtain an isomorphism

(3.2)

{\operatorname{Anan}}_{e;-L_{1},\ldots,-L_{n}}\coloneqq\Sigma^{e-\oplus_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}^{\vee}-\oplus_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}}{\operatorname{Anan}}_{L_{1}^{\vee},\ldots,L_{n}^{\vee}}:\Sigma^{e-\sum_{i=1}^{n}[L_{i}]}1_{X}\xrightarrow{\sim}\\
\Sigma^{e-\sum_{i=1}^{n}[L_{i}^{\vee}]}1_{X}.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let us suppose
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
irreducible, and use the shorthand
{\operatorname{Anan}}\coloneqq{\operatorname{Anan}}_{e;-L_{1},\ldots,-L_{n}}
for the isomorphism (
3.2
). Let also
d\coloneqq{\operatorname{rank}}(e)-n
. For
p:X\to{\operatorname{Spec}}k
in
{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
, the diagram in
{\operatorname{Mod}}_{p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{e-\sum_{i=1}^{n}[L_{i}]-d[\mathcal{O}_{X}]}1_{X}\wedge p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{e-\sum_{i=1}^{n}[L_{i}^{\vee}]-d[\mathcal{O}_{X}]}1_{X}\wedge p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{-2d,-d}{\operatorname{Anan}}\wedge{\operatorname{id}}_{p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{th}}_{H{\mathbb{Z}}{\operatorname{Mod}}}^{f}(e-\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-1)^{n}{\operatorname{th}}^{f}_{H{\mathbb{Z}}{\operatorname{Mod}}}(e-\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}^{\vee})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

commutes.

Proof.

By using a Jouanolou cover of
X
, we may assume that
X
is affine. In that case, there exists a vector bundle
E
over
X
such that
-e=[E]-s[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]
, and let
r\coloneqq s-d
. In this way, we are reduced to prove the commutativity of the diagram

(3.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{2r,r}\Sigma^{-[E+\oplus_{i}^{n}L_{i}]}1_{X}\wedge p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{2r,r}\Sigma^{-[E+\oplus_{i}^{n}L_{i}^{\vee}]}1_{X}\wedge p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{2r,r}{\operatorname{Anan}}_{E;L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}}\wedge{\operatorname{id}}_{p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{th}}^{f}_{H{\mathbb{Z}}{\operatorname{Mod}}}(-E-\oplus_{i}^{n}L_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-1)^{n}{\operatorname{th}}^{f}_{H{\mathbb{Z}}{\operatorname{Mod}}}(-E-\oplus_{i}^{n}L_{i}^{\vee})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

in
{\operatorname{Mod}}_{p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}}
. Let
\rho:{\operatorname{Th}}(E+\oplus_{i=1}^{n}L_{i})\xrightarrow{\sim}{\operatorname{Th}}(E+\oplus_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}^{\vee})
the isomorphism in
{\mathcal{H}}_{\bullet}(k)
defined by Ananyevskiy.

The commutativity of (
3.3
) is equivalent to

(\Sigma^{2r,r}{\operatorname{Anan}}_{E;L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}}\wedge{\operatorname{id}}_{p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}})^{*}{\operatorname{th}}_{H{\mathbb{Z}}}^{f}(E\oplus_{i}^{n}L_{i}^{\vee})=(-1)^{n}{\operatorname{th}}_{H{\mathbb{Z}}}^{f}(E+\oplus_{i}^{n}L_{i}),

and this is in turn equivalent to

\rho^{*}{\operatorname{th}}^{H{\mathbb{Z}}}(E+\oplus_{i}^{n}L_{i}^{\vee})=(-1)^{n}{\operatorname{th}}^{H{\mathbb{Z}}}(E+\oplus_{i}^{n}L_{i}).

Lemma
3.2
below shows in detail the computation for
n=1
. The result immediately follows by induction on
n
.
∎

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
irreducible. Let
E\to X
a vector bundle of finite rank over
X
and
L\to X
a line bundle over
X
, with dual bundle
L^{\vee}\to X
. Let
\rho:{\operatorname{Th}}(E\oplus L)\xrightarrow{\sim}{\operatorname{Th}}(E\oplus L^{\vee})
the isomorphism in
{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
defined as in
[
2
, Lemma 4.1]
. Then
\rho^{*}{\operatorname{th}}^{H\mathbb{Z}}(E+L^{\vee})=-{\operatorname{th}}^{H\mathbb{Z}}(E+L).

Proof.

Since we are working in the stable setting, we simplify the notation by writing
\rho
for
\Sigma^{\infty}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}\rho:p_{\#}\Sigma^{E+L}1_{X}\xrightarrow{\sim}p_{\#}\Sigma^{E+L^{\vee}}1_{X}
, with
p\coloneqq p_{X}:X\to{\operatorname{Spec}}k
the structure map. Let us briefly review the construction of
\rho
from
[
2
]
.

Let
\pi_{L}:L\to X
and
\pi_{L^{\vee}}:L^{\vee}\to X
the structure morphisms of the two vector bundles. Let us denote by
L^{0}
and
(L^{\vee})^{0}
the complements of the zero sections, and by
\pi_{L^{0}}
,
\pi_{(L^{\vee})^{0}}
the restrictions of the relative morphisms. Let us note that
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-invariance (Proposition
2.11
) implies
\pi_{L\#}1_{L}\simeq 1_{X}\simeq\pi_{L^{\vee}\#}1_{L^{\vee}}
. The localization sequence associated to the inclusion
L^{0}\hookrightarrow L
as in (
2.6
) gives a distinguished triangle

(3.4)

\pi_{L^{0}\#}1_{L^{0}}\to 1_{X}\to\Sigma^{L}1_{X}\to\pi_{L^{0}\#}1_{L^{0}}[1],

and analogously, we have the distinguished triangle

(3.5)

\pi_{(L^{\vee})^{0}\#}1_{(L^{\vee})^{0}}\to 1_{X}\to\Sigma^{L^{\vee}}1_{X}\to\pi_{(L^{\vee})^{0}\#}1_{(L^{\vee})^{0}}[1].

There is a unique isomorphism of
X
-schemes
f:L^{0}\xrightarrow{\sim}(L^{\vee})^{0}
given by taking, fiberwise, the vector
v
to the functional
f_{v}
such that
f_{v}(v)=1
. By taking
\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{\infty}f
in
{\operatorname{SH}}(X)
, this gives an isomorphism between the left hand terms of (
3.4
) and (
3.5
), that, together with
{\operatorname{id}}_{X}
, induces the isomorphism
{\operatorname{Anan}}_{L}:\Sigma^{L}1_{X}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Sigma^{L^{\vee}}1_{X}
on the cofibers. Applying
p_{\#}\circ\Sigma^{E}
to
{\operatorname{Anan}}_{L}
gives the isomorphism
\rho:p_{\#}\Sigma^{E+L}1_{X}\xrightarrow{\sim}p_{\#}\Sigma^{E+L^{\vee}}1_{X}
.

From this construction, we see that
\rho={\operatorname{id}}_{p_{\#}\Sigma^{E}1_{X}}\wedge\rho_{L}
, with
\rho_{L}\coloneqq p_{\#}{\operatorname{Anan}}_{L}
, and since Thom classes are additive, we have
\rho^{*}{\operatorname{th}}^{H\mathbb{Z}}(E+L^{\vee})={\operatorname{th}}^{H{\mathbb{Z}}}(E)\cup\rho_{L}^{*}{\operatorname{th}}^{H{\mathbb{Z}}}(L^{\vee})
. This reduces to the case
E=0
and
\rho=\rho_{L}
.

Next, we reduce to the case
X={\operatorname{Spec}}k(X)
. The Thom isomorphism in
H\mathbb{Z}
-cohomology makes
H\mathbb{Z}^{**}(p_{\#}\Sigma^{L}1_{X})
and
H\mathbb{Z}^{**}(p_{\#}\Sigma^{L^{\vee}}1_{X})
two free
H\mathbb{Z}^{**}(X)
-modules of rank
1
, generated respectively by
{\operatorname{th}}^{H\mathbb{Z}}(L)
and
{\operatorname{th}}^{H\mathbb{Z}}(L^{\vee})
, both in bidegree
(2,1)
.
\rho_{L}^{*}
, as an isomorphism of
H\mathbb{Z}^{**}(X)
-modules, sends generators to generators, then
\rho_{L}^{*}{\operatorname{th}}^{H\mathbb{Z}}(L^{\vee})=\alpha\cdot{\operatorname{th}}^{H\mathbb{Z}}(L)
, with
\alpha\in H\mathbb{Z}^{0,0}(X)=\mathbb{Z}.
Since
X
is irreducible, there is a unique generic point
i:{\operatorname{Spec}}k(X)\to X
. Let then

\rho_{i^{*}L}:p_{k(X)\#}\Sigma^{i^{*}L}1_{k(X)}\xrightarrow{\sim}p_{k(X)\#}\Sigma^{i^{*}L^{\vee}}1_{k(X)}

be the isomorphism obtained in the same way as
\rho
, with
p_{k(X)}\coloneqq p\circ i={\operatorname{Spec}}(k\hookrightarrow k(X))
. We have the commutative diagram

\textstyle{H{\mathbb{Z}}^{0,0}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{H{\mathbb{Z}}^{0,0}(k(X))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[1_{X},p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\cup\;{\operatorname{th}}^{H\mathbb{Z}}(L)}
\textstyle{[1_{k(X)},p_{k(X)}^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(k(X))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\cup\;{\operatorname{th}}^{p_{k(X)}^{*}H\mathbb{Z}}(i^{*}L)}
\textstyle{[\Sigma^{L}1_{X},\Sigma^{2,1}p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{Th}}(i)^{*}}
\textstyle{[\Sigma^{i^{*}L}1_{k(X)},\Sigma^{2,1}p_{k(X)}^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(k(X))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H{\mathbb{Z}}^{2,1}({\operatorname{Th}}(L))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{Th}}(i)^{*}}
\textstyle{H{\mathbb{Z}}^{2,1}({\operatorname{Th}}(i^{*}L))}

and a similar diagram for
L^{\vee}
. Let
\alpha_{k(X)}\in{\mathbb{Z}}=H{\mathbb{Z}}^{0,0}(k(X))
be the element such that
\rho^{*}_{i^{*}L}({\operatorname{th}}^{p_{k(X)}^{*}H\mathbb{Z}}(i^{*}L^{\vee}))=\alpha_{k(X)}\cdot{\operatorname{th}}^{p_{k(X)}^{*}H\mathbb{Z}}(i^{*}L)
. The maps
\rho^{*}_{L}
and
\rho^{*}_{i^{*}L}
give a map to the above diagram from the corresponding one for
L^{\vee}
, so we have
i^{*}\alpha=\alpha_{k(X)}
. But since
i^{*}:{\mathbb{Z}}=H{\mathbb{Z}}^{0,0}(X)\to H{\mathbb{Z}}^{0,0}(k(X))={\mathbb{Z}}
is the identity map on
{\mathbb{Z}}
, we have
\alpha_{k(X)}=\alpha
. This reduces to the case
X={\operatorname{Spec}}k(X)
.

Changing notation, we may simply assume that
X={\operatorname{Spec}}k
.
L
is then a one dimensional
k
-vector space with zero section being the origin. The total space of
L
is (non canonically) isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{1}
. Let us choose any isomorphism
L\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{A}^{1}
. This also induces an isomorphism
L^{\vee}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{A}^{1}
through
f\mapsto f(1)
. Through these isomorphisms, we identify
L^{0}
and
(L^{\vee})^{0}
with
\mathbb{G}_{m}
. The isomorphism of schemes
L^{0}\xrightarrow{\sim}(L^{\vee})^{0}
given by
v\mapsto f_{v}
, induces the automorphism
t\mapsto t^{-1}
of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
. The isomorphisms
L\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{A}^{1}\xleftarrow{\sim}L^{\vee}
induce

\Sigma^{L}1_{X}\xrightarrow{\sim}S^{1}\wedge\mathbb{G}_{m}\xleftarrow{\sim}\Sigma^{L^{\vee}}1_{X},

and through these, the isomorphism
\rho_{L}
can be read as the automorphism
{\operatorname{id}}_{S^{1}}\wedge(t\mapsto t^{-1})
of
S^{1}\wedge{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
, where
t
is the canonical coordinate on
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
. We have

H{\mathbb{Z}}^{2,1}({\mathbb{P}}^{1})\cong H\mathbb{Z}^{2,1}(S^{1}\wedge{\mathbb{G}}_{m})\cong H\mathbb{Z}^{1,1}({\mathbb{G}}_{m})\xleftarrow{\sim}\mathbb{Z},

with the last isomorphism sending
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
to
t^{n}\in H\mathbb{Z}^{1,1}({\mathbb{G}}_{m}))
. Thus,
\rho_{L}
induces the isomorphism
n\mapsto-n
on
{\mathbb{Z}}\cong H\mathbb{Z}^{2,1}({\mathbb{P}}^{1})
.

{\operatorname{th}}^{H\mathbb{Z}(L)}
and
{\operatorname{th}}^{H\mathbb{Z}}(L^{\vee})
belong to
H\mathbb{Z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{P}^{1})\cong\mathbb{Z}
, and they are generators. Since
L\simeq L^{\vee}\simeq{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{F}
as line bundles over
{\operatorname{Spec}}F
, we have
{\operatorname{th}}^{H\mathbb{Z}}(L)={\operatorname{th}}^{H\mathbb{Z}}(L^{\vee})
, and since
\rho_{L}^{*}(n)=-n
, we conclude
\rho_{L}^{*}({\operatorname{th}}^{H\mathbb{Z}}(L^{\vee}))=-{\operatorname{th}}^{H\mathbb{Z}}(L)
.
∎

Let us now consider
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
and
e=\sum_{i=1}^{n}[L_{i}]-[T_{X}]+d_{X}[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]\in K_{0}(X)
, with
d_{X}\coloneqq{\operatorname{dim}}_{k}(X)
. Let us suppose
v\in K_{0}(X)
being an element satisfying the identity

(3.6)

v=e-\sum_{i=1}^{n}[L_{i}^{\vee}]-d_{X}[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}].

In particular,
{\operatorname{rank}}(v)=-{\operatorname{rank}}(T_{X})=-d_{X}
. In this case, (
3.2
) gives the isomorphism

(3.7)

{\operatorname{Anan}}_{-L_{1},\ldots,-L_{n}}(v):\Sigma^{-T_{X}+{\operatorname{rank}}(T_{X})[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]}1_{X}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Sigma^{v-{\operatorname{rank}}(v)[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]}1_{X},

where
{\operatorname{Anan}}_{-L_{1},\ldots,-L_{n}}(v):={\operatorname{Anan}}_{e;-L_{1},\ldots,-L_{n}}
.

Definition 3.3
.

With
p:X\to{\operatorname{Spec}}k
smooth and proper of dimension
d_{X}
, and
v\in K_{0}(X)
as in (
3.6
), we have the
twisted degree

{\operatorname{deg}}^{\operatorname{Anan}}_{k}:[1_{X},\Sigma^{2d_{X},d_{X}}p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(X)}\to{\mathbb{Z}}

defined as the composition (
3.8
) below, where we write
{\operatorname{Anan}}
for the isomorphism (
3.7
):

(3.8)

[1_{X},\Sigma^{2d_{X},d_{X}}p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(X)}\xrightarrow{\sim}[p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}},\Sigma^{2d_{X},d_{X}}p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{Mod}}_{p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}}}\\
\xrightarrow[\sim]{{\operatorname{th}}_{H{\mathbb{Z}}{\operatorname{Mod}}}^{f}(v)^{*}}[\Sigma^{v-{\operatorname{rank}}(v)[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]}1_{X}\wedge p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}},\Sigma^{2d_{X},d_{X}}p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{Mod}}_{p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}}}\\
\xrightarrow[\sim]{({\operatorname{Anan}}\wedge{\operatorname{id}})^{*}}[\Sigma^{-T_{X}+{\operatorname{rank}}(T_{X})[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]}1_{X}\wedge p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}},\Sigma^{2d_{X},d_{X}}p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{Mod}}_{p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}}}\\
=[\Sigma^{-T_{X}}1_{X}\wedge p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}},p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{Mod}}_{p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}}}=[\Sigma^{-T_{X}}1_{X},p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(X)}=[p_{\#}(\Sigma^{-T_{X}}1_{X}),H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(k)}\\
\xrightarrow{(p^{\vee})^{*}}[1_{k},H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(k)}=H{\mathbb{Z}}^{0,0}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)={\mathbb{Z}}.

Proposition 3.4
.

Suppose we have
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
irreducible,
v\in K_{0}(X)
and line bundles
L_{1},\ldots,L_{r}
such that Ananyevskiy’s isomorphism
{\operatorname{Anan}}_{L_{1},\ldots,L_{r}}
is defined. Suppose that
X
is smooth and proper of dimension
d_{X}
over
k
. Then, for
\alpha\in H{\mathbb{Z}}^{2d_{X},d_{X}}(X)
we have
{\operatorname{deg}}_{k}(\alpha)=(-1)^{r}{\operatorname{deg}}^{\operatorname{Anan}}_{k}(\alpha).

Proof.

By rewriting the construction of the proper pushforward, we can see
{\operatorname{deg}}_{k}:H{\mathbb{Z}}^{2d_{X},d_{X}}(X)\to{\mathbb{Z}}
as the composition

(3.9)

H{\mathbb{Z}}^{2d_{X},d_{X}}(X)=[1_{X},\Sigma^{2d_{X},d_{X}}p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(X)}\xrightarrow{\sim}[p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}},\Sigma^{2d_{X},d_{X}}p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{Mod}}_{p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}}}\\
\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{th}}_{H{\mathbb{Z}}{\operatorname{Mod}}}^{f}(-T_{X})^{*}}[\Sigma^{-T_{X}+d_{X}[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]}1_{X}\wedge p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}},\Sigma^{2d_{X},d_{X}}p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{Mod}}_{p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}}}\\
=[\Sigma^{-T_{X}}1_{X}\wedge p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}},p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{Mod}}_{p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}}}=[\Sigma^{-T_{X}}1_{X},p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(X)}\\
=[p_{\#}(\Sigma^{-T_{X}}1_{X}),H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(k)}\xrightarrow{(p^{\vee})^{*}}[1_{k},H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(k)}=H{\mathbb{Z}}^{0,0}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)={\mathbb{Z}}.

Comparing (
3.9
) with (
3.8
) and applying Lemma
3.1
concludes the proof.
∎

We want to apply this comparison to a certain kind of motivic cohomology classes. We need a preliminary definition.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
be an oriented ring spectrum,
c\in H{\mathbb{Z}}^{a,b}({\mathcal{E}})=[{\mathcal{E}},\Sigma^{a,b}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(k)}
, and
v\in K_{0}(X)
for
X\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
. Let
p:X\to{\operatorname{Spec}}k
be the structure map. Applying
p^{*}
to
c
gives the map
p^{*}(c):p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\to\Sigma^{a,b}p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}
. The Thom classes for
{\mathcal{E}}
give us the map
{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}(v):\Sigma^{v-{\operatorname{rank}}(v)[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]}1_{X}\to p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}
.
We have the map
\tilde{c}^{\mathcal{E}}(v)^{\prime}
defined as the composition

\Sigma^{v-{\operatorname{rank}}(v)[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]}1_{X}\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}(v)}p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\xrightarrow{p^{*}(c)}\Sigma^{a,b}p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}

in
{\operatorname{SH}}(X)
, and by the adjunction
p_{\#}\dashv p^{*}
, the map

\tilde{c}^{\mathcal{E}}(v):p_{\#}\Sigma^{v-{\operatorname{rank}}(v)[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]}1_{X}\to p_{\#}p^{*}\Sigma^{a,b}H{\mathbb{Z}}\to\Sigma^{a,b}H{\mathbb{Z}},

which can be seen as an element of
H{\mathbb{Z}}^{a,b}(p_{\#}\Sigma^{v-{\operatorname{rank}}(v)[{\mathcal{O}}_{X}]}1_{X})
. Via the isomorphism
\Sigma^{a,b}{\operatorname{th}}_{H{\mathbb{Z}}}^{f}(-v)
,
\tilde{c}^{\mathcal{E}}(v)
gives an element
c^{\mathcal{E}}(v)\in H{\mathbb{Z}}^{a,b}(X).

Corollary 3.6
.

Let
Y\in{\operatorname{Sm}}/k
be proper of dimension
d
over
k
and irreducible, and let
(v,\vartheta)
be a stable twist of
-T_{Y}
. Let us suppose that, for a certain
e\in K_{0}(Y)
and
L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}
line bundles on
Y
, we have
v=e-\sum_{i=1}^{s}[L_{i}^{\vee}]
,
-[T_{Y}]=e-\sum_{i=1}^{n}[L_{i}]
in
K_{0}(Y)
, and
\vartheta
is the isomorphism

\Sigma^{-2d,-d}{\operatorname{Anan}}_{e,-L_{1},\ldots,-L_{n}}(v):\Sigma^{-T_{Y}}1_{Y}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Sigma^{v}1_{Y}.

If
{\mathcal{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
is an oriented commutative ring spectrum, and
c
a class in
H{\mathbb{Z}}^{2d,d}({\mathcal{E}})
,then

{\operatorname{deg}}_{k}c^{\mathcal{E}}(v)=(-1)^{n}c\circ[Y,v,\vartheta]_{\mathcal{E}}\in H{\mathbb{Z}}^{0,0}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)={\mathbb{Z}}.

Proof.

Let
p:=p_{Y}:Y\to{\operatorname{Spec}}k
be the structure map. We write
{\operatorname{Anan}}
for
{\operatorname{Anan}}_{e,-L_{1},\ldots,-L_{n}}(v)
.

By looking at the definition of
[Y,v,\vartheta]_{\mathcal{E}}
(Definition
2.33
), we see that
c\circ[Y,v,\vartheta]
is the composition

1_{k}\xrightarrow{p^{\vee}}p_{\#}\Sigma^{-T_{Y}}1_{Y}\xrightarrow[\sim]{p_{\#}\Sigma^{-2d,-d}{\operatorname{Anan}}}p_{\#}\Sigma^{v}1_{Y}\xrightarrow{\Sigma^{-2d,-d}{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}(v)^{\prime}}\Sigma^{-2d,-d}{\mathcal{E}}\xrightarrow{\Sigma^{-2d-d}c}H{\mathbb{Z}},

where
{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}(v)^{\prime}
is the map corresponding to
{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}(v)
by adjunction
p_{\#}\dashv p^{*}
.

On the other hand, the composition
p_{\#}\Sigma^{v}1_{Y}\xrightarrow{\Sigma^{-2d,-d}{\operatorname{th}}_{\mathcal{E}}(v)^{\prime}}\Sigma^{-2d,-d}{\mathcal{E}}\xrightarrow{\Sigma^{-2d-d}c}H{\mathbb{Z}}
is the class
\Sigma^{-2d,-d}\tilde{c}^{\mathcal{E}}(v)
(see Definition
3.5
), then

c\circ[Y,v,\vartheta]=(p^{\vee})^{*}(\Sigma^{-2d,-d}(p_{\#}{\operatorname{Anan}})^{*}(\Sigma^{-2d,-d}\tilde{c}^{\mathcal{E}}(v))).

We also see from Definition
3.5
that the isomorphism
H{\mathbb{Z}}^{2d,2}(p_{\#}\Sigma^{v+d[{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}]}1_{Y})\simeq H{\mathbb{Z}}^{2d,d}(Y)
giving
c^{\mathcal{E}}(v)
is induced by the isomorphism
{\operatorname{th}}_{H{\mathbb{Z}}}^{f}(v)
, or equivalently, by
{\operatorname{th}}_{H{\mathbb{Z}}}^{f}(v)^{*}
.

Lastly, the isomorphism
p_{\#}{\operatorname{Anan}}
in
{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
corresponds to the isomorphism

{\operatorname{Anan}}\wedge{\operatorname{id}}_{p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}}:\Sigma^{-T_{Y}+d[{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}]}1_{Y}\wedge p_{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Sigma^{v+d[{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}]}1_{Y}\wedge p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}

in
{\operatorname{Mod}}_{p^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}}
through the free-forgetful adjunction.

Thus, by looking at the composition (
3.8
), we see that
c\circ[Y,v,\vartheta]
is the class
{\operatorname{deg}}_{k}^{\operatorname{Anan}}(c^{\mathcal{E}}(v))
in
H{\mathbb{Z}}^{0,0}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)
. Applying Proposition
3.4
concludes the proof.
∎

4.
Cellular structure of
{\operatorname{MSp}}

Definition 4.1
.

We denote by
\langle 1_{k}\rangle
the full localizing subcategory of
{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
generated by the motivic spheres, or in other words, the smallest full subcategory containing
1_{k}
and and closed with respect to coproducts
\oplus
, homotopy colimits, and suspensions
\Sigma^{a,b}
for
a,b\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. We will say that a motivic spectrum
{\mathbb{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
is
cellular
if it belongs to the subcategory
\langle 1_{k}\rangle
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbb{E}}\to{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime\prime}
be a cofiber sequence in
{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
such that two of
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}
,
{\mathbb{E}}
and
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime\prime}
are cellular. Then so is the third.

Proof.

If
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}
and
{\mathbb{E}}
are cellular, so is
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime\prime}
, since
\langle 1_{k}\rangle
is closed with respect to homotopy colimits, and in particular homotopy cofibers. For the other two cases, we note that
{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}
is the homotopy cofiber of
\Sigma^{-1,0}{\mathbb{E}}\to\Sigma^{-1,0}{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime\prime}
, and
{\mathbb{E}}
is the homotopy cofiber of
\Sigma^{-1,0}{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime\prime}\to{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}
. In both cases, the result follows because
\langle 1_{k}\rangle
is closed under
\Sigma^{-1,0}
-suspensions and homotopy cofibers.
∎

Example 4.3
.

The algebraic cobordism spectrum
{\operatorname{MGL}}
is cellular (see
[
9
, Theorem 6.4]
or the proof of
[
22
, Theorem 6.11]
). In this case, the cellularity is induced by the Schubert cell decomposition of Grassmannians.

To prove the cellularity of
{\operatorname{MSp}}
, we need to go through the geometry of quaternionic Grassmannians and their vector bundles. We noted that almost the same procedure was used in
[
40
, Proposition 3.1]
.

4.1.
Vector bundles over quaternionic Grassmannians

Let
V
be a
(2n)
-dimensional vector space over
k
, and
\phi
the symplectic form on
V
represented by the standard symplectic matrix
=\phi_{2n}
(see example
2.52
(1)) with respect to a fixed basis
\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{n},\ldots,y_{1}\rangle
.

Over
{\operatorname{Spec}}k
,
{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)
can be seen as the classifying space of the
(2r)
-dimensional symplectic vector subspaces of the symplectic vector space
(V,\phi)
.

We denote by
E_{i}
the
i
-dimensional subspace
\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{i}\rangle
of
V
. Following
[
33
, Theorem 4.1]
, we define
N^{+}\coloneqq{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)\cap{\operatorname{Gr}}(2r,E_{1}^{\perp})
.
N^{+}
is a closed subscheme of
{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)
, and it is a rank
2r
subbundle of the normal bundle
N_{i}
of the obvious inclusion
i:{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n-1)\to{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)
. More specifically,
[
33
, Theorem 4.1 (b)]
gives
N_{i}=N^{+}\oplus N^{-}
, where
N^{-}={\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)\cap{\operatorname{Gr}}(2r,y_{1}^{\perp})
. Moreover, by
[
33
, Theorem 4.1 (c)]
, both
N^{+}
and
N^{-}
are isomorphic to the tautological rank
2r
symplectic bundle over
{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n-1)
. In particular

{\operatorname{dim}}(N^{+})={\operatorname{dim}}({\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n-1))+2r=4nr-2r-4r^{2}.
Finally, we denote by
Y
the open complement of the closed subscheme
N^{+}
in
{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)
. Before proving our main statement on the Thom spaces of vector bundles over
{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)
, we need to study the geometry of the open stratum
Y
, following the ideas of
[
33
]
.

Y
can be seen as the classifying space of
2r
-dimensional subspaces of
V
, symplectic with respect to the form
\phi
, that are not subspaces of
\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{n},\ldots y_{2}\rangle
. Let us consider the restriction of the tautological rank
2r
bundle
\mathcal{U}\to Y
, with
\mathcal{U}\coloneqq E^{\operatorname{Sp}}_{2r,2n}\mid_{Y}
. Note that a point in
\mathcal{U}
is a pair
(y,v)
where
y
is a point of
Y
, hence representing a vector subspace
[y]
, and
v
a vector in
[y]
. Let
\lambda:\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{O}_{S}
be the map that takes
(y,(v_{1},\ldots,v_{2n}))\in\mathcal{U}
to
v_{2n}
, and let
Y_{1}\coloneqq\lambda^{-1}(1)
. Let
g_{1}:Y_{1}\to Y
be the induced map.

Let now

Y_{2}\coloneqq\{(y,e,f)\mid y\in Y,\;e\in[y],\;f\in[y],\;e_{2n}=0,\;f_{2n}=1,\;\phi(e,f)=1\},

and let
g_{2}:Y_{2}\to Y_{1}
be the map defined by
(y,e,f)\mapsto(y,f)
.

Lemma 4.4
.

The maps
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
are Zariski-locally trivial affine space bundles.

Proof.

Since
Y_{1}=\lambda^{-1}(1)
, the map
g_{1}
is the restriction of the projection
\mathcal{U}\to Y
to a Zariski closed subscheme.
{\operatorname{Ker}}\lambda
is a subbundle of rank
2r-1
of
\mathcal{U}
, and fiberwise, it acts freely on
\mathcal{U}
by translations. Since this action preserves the image of
\lambda
, it induces an action on
Y_{1}
. Since the action is fiberwise it preserves
Y
, and
Y
is obtained as the quotient of
Y_{1}
by this action.
\lambda
is surjective by construction, thus,
Y_{1}\to Y
is a torsor for the vector subbundle
{\operatorname{Ker}}\lambda
, and then
g_{1}
is a Zariski-locally trivial affine space bundle with fibre
\mathbb{A}^{2r-1}
.

In order to obtain the statement for
g_{2}
, let us consider the rank
2r-1
vector bundle
g_{1}^{*}{\operatorname{Ker}}\lambda\to Y_{1}
. A point in this is given by a triple
(y,e,f)
with
y
in
Y
, and
e,f
two vectors in
[y]
, with
\lambda(f)=1
and
\lambda(e)=0
. We then get a surjective map of vector bundles

\displaystyle\lambda^{\prime}:g_{1}^{*}{\operatorname{Ker}}\lambda

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{O}_{S}

\displaystyle(y,e,f)

\displaystyle\mapsto\psi(e,f),

and
Y_{2}=\lambda^{\prime-1}(1)
.
Y_{2}
is then a Zariski closed subscheme of the vector bundle
g_{1}^{-1}{\operatorname{Ker}}\lambda
over
Y_{1}
, and
{\operatorname{Ker}}\lambda^{\prime}
is a subbundle of rank
2r-2
that acts fiberwise on
g_{1}^{-1}{\operatorname{Ker}}\lambda
by translations. The induced map
Y_{2}\to Y_{1}
is the map
g_{2}
. By applying the previous argument to this situation, we obtain that
g_{2}
is a Zariski locally trivial affine space bundle with fiber
\mathbb{A}^{2r-2}
.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\tilde{Y}
be the space

\tilde{Y}\coloneqq\{(e,f)\in V\times V\mid e_{2n}=0,\;f_{2n}=1,\;\phi(e,f)=1\}.

Then, there is an isomorphism of
k
-schemes
Y_{2}\simeq\tilde{Y}\times{\operatorname{HGr}}(r-1,n-1)
.

Proof.

We can write
V
as
\langle x_{1}\rangle\oplus V^{\prime}\oplus\langle y_{1}\rangle
, with
V^{\prime}=\langle x_{2},\ldots,x_{n},y_{n},\ldots,y_{2}\rangle
.
V^{\prime}
equipped with the symplectic structure induced by
\phi
is a symplectic vector subspace of
V
, and we can identify
{\operatorname{HGr}}(r-1,n-1)
with
{\operatorname{HGr}}(r-1,V^{\prime})
. A point of
\tilde{Y}\times{\operatorname{HGr}}(r-1,n-1)
is then a triple
(e,f,x)
with
e,f
vectors of
V
satisfying the three defining conditions of
\tilde{Y}
, and
x
representing a symplectic subspace
[x]
of
V^{\prime}
of rank
2r-2
. One can associate to the pair of vectors
(e,f)
two invertible matrices

\rho_{e}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0&&\ldots&&&&0\\
e_{2}&1&0&&&&&&0\\
e_{3}&0&1&0&&0&&&\ldots\\
\ldots&&&1&&&&&\ldots\\
\ldots&&&&1&&&&\ldots\\
\ldots&&0&&&1&&&\ldots\\
e_{2n-2}&&&&&&1&&0\\
e_{2n-1}&&&&&\ldots&0&1&0\\
0&-e_{2n-1}&-e_{2n-2}&\ldots&-e_{n+1}&e_{n}&\ldots&e_{2}&1\end{pmatrix},

\rho_{f}=\begin{pmatrix}1&-f_{2n-1}&-f_{2n-2}&\ldots&-f_{n+1}&f_{n}&\ldots&f_{2}&f_{1}\\
0&1&0&&&&&&f_{2}\\
0&0&1&0&&&0&&f_{3}\\
\ldots&&&1&&&&&\\
\ldots&&&&1&&&&\ldots\\
\ldots&&0&&&1&&&\ldots\\
\ldots&&&&&&1&&\\
0&&&&&&0&1&f_{2n-1}\\
0&&&\ldots&&&&0&1\end{pmatrix}.

Let now
\rho_{e,f}\coloneqq\rho_{f}\cdot\rho_{e}
. We get
\rho_{e,f}(x_{1})=\rho_{f}(1,e_{2},\ldots,e_{2n-1},0)=e
, and
\rho_{e,f}(y_{1})=\rho_{f}(y_{1})=f
. It is immediate to see that
\rho_{e,f}
preserves the form
\phi
. Thus, for each symplectic subspace
W
of
V
,
\rho_{e,f}
maps
W^{\perp}
isometrically to
(\rho_{e,f}(W))^{\perp}
. In particular,
\rho_{e,f}(V^{\prime})=\rho_{e,f}(\langle x_{1},y_{1}\rangle^{\perp})\simeq\langle e,f\rangle^{\perp}
. We can then define a map:

\displaystyle\gamma:\tilde{Y}\times{\operatorname{HGr}}(r-1,n-1)

\displaystyle\to Y_{2}

\displaystyle((e,f),x)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\rho_{e,f}([x])\oplus\langle e\rangle\oplus\langle f\rangle,e,f),

and note that this is an isomorphism with inverse

\displaystyle\gamma^{-1}:\;\;Y_{2}

\displaystyle\to\tilde{Y}\times{\operatorname{HGr}}(r-1,n-1)

\displaystyle(y,e,f)

\displaystyle\mapsto((e,f),\rho_{e,f}^{-1}([y])\cap V^{\prime}).

∎

We get a map
q:Y_{2}\to{\operatorname{HGr}}(r-1,n-1)
by identifying
Y_{2}
with
\tilde{Y}\times{\operatorname{HGr}}(r-1,n-1)
thanks to Lemma
4.5
, and applying the canonical projection. Moreover,
\tilde{Y}
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{A}}^{4n-3}
, with inverse given by:

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}^{4n-3}

\displaystyle\to\tilde{Y}

\displaystyle(x_{1},\ldots,x_{4n-3})

\displaystyle\mapsto(e_{1},x_{1},\ldots,x_{2n-2},0,x_{2n-1},x_{2n},\ldots,x_{4n-3},1),

where
e_{1}=1-\phi_{2n-2}((x_{1},\ldots,x_{2n-2}),(x_{2n},\ldots,x_{4n-3}))
. The map
q
is then a trivial
\mathbb{A}^{4n-3}
-bundle, and as such, it is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-equivalence.

We summarize our discussion about the open stratum
Y
in the following proposition.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
Y={\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)\setminus N^{+}
. Then we have a zig-zag diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{HGr}}(r-1,n-1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

where the maps
g_{1},g_{2}
and
q
are Zariski locally trivial affine space bundles, hence
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalences. Moreover,
q
is a trivial affine space bundle with fiber
\mathbb{A}^{4n-3}
.

We can now prove the main fact of this subsection.

Proposition 4.7
.

For
v\in K_{0}({\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n))
,
p_{{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)\#}\Sigma^{v}1_{{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
is cellular.

Proof.

We prove the statement for all
n\geq r\geq 0
by induction on
r
.

For
r=0
,
{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)
is isomorphic to
{\operatorname{Spec}}k
, and the statement is immediate. We can then assume
r>0
and proceed by induction on
n\geq r
.

Again, the case
n=r
is trivial, so we assume
n>r+1
.

We rename the closed subscheme
N^{+}
of
{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)
as
Z
, and again
Y
denotes its open complement. Since
Z\to{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n-1)
is a rank
2r
vector bundle,
Z
is smooth over
k
of codimension
2r
in
{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)
. Let
p
,
p_{Z}
,
p_{Y}
denote the structure maps of
{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)
,
Z
and
Y
.

From the diagram

(4.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

we get the localization distinguished triangle

(4.2)

j_{!}j^{!}\to{\operatorname{id}}_{{\operatorname{SH}}({\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n))}\to i_{*}i^{*}\to j_{!}j^{!}[1]

of endofunctors of
{\operatorname{SH}}({\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n))
, by Proposition
2.14
. By composing this sequence with
p_{\#}
and applying it to
\Sigma^{v}1_{{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)}
, we get the cofiber sequence in
{\operatorname{SH}}(k)

(4.3)

p_{\#}j_{!}j^{!}\Sigma^{v}1_{{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)}\to p_{\#}\Sigma^{v}1_{{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)}\to p_{\#}i_{*}i^{*}\Sigma^{v}1_{{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)}.

Thanks to Proposition
4.2
, it is enough show that the left-hand term and the right-hand term of (
4.3
) belong to
\langle 1_{k}\rangle
.

Let us see the left-hand term first. Because
j
is an open immersion, we have
j_{!}\simeq j_{\#}
and
j^{!}\simeq j^{*}
, then

p_{\#}j_{!}j^{!}\Sigma^{v}1_{{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)}\simeq p_{Y\#}j^{*}\Sigma^{v}1_{{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)}\simeq p_{Y\#}\Sigma^{j^{*}v}1_{Y}.

Proposition
4.6
gives the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{HGr}}(r-1,n-1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

where the maps
g_{1},g_{2}
and
q
are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalences, in fact, affine space bundles. Moreover,
q
is a trivial
\mathbb{A}^{4n-3}
-bundle, hence, it has a zero section
s_{0}:{\operatorname{HGr}}(r-1,n-1)\to Y_{2}
, which is an inverse of
q
in
{\mathcal{H}}(k)
. The composition
h\coloneqq g_{1}\circ g_{2}\circ s_{0}:{\operatorname{HGr}}(r-1,n-1)\to Y
is thus an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-weak equivalence. Now,
g_{1},g_{2}
and
q
are affine space bundles, and
Y_{1}
,
Y_{2}
and

{\operatorname{HGr}}(r-1,n-1)
are smooth, hence, by
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-homotopy invariance of
K
-theory on regular schemes
[
34
, §7 paragraph 1, and Proposition 4.1]
, the maps
q^{*}:K_{0}({\operatorname{HGr}}(r-1,n-1))\to K_{0}(Y_{2})
and
(g_{1}g_{2})^{*}:K_{0}(Y)\to K_{0}(Y_{2})
are isomorphisms. Thus, there is an element
v^{\prime}\in K_{0}({\operatorname{HGr}}(r-1,n-1))
such that
q^{*}v^{\prime}=(g_{1}g_{2})^{*}j^{*}v
in
K_{0}(Y_{2})
, and, since
g_{1},g_{2}
and
q
are
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
weak equivalences, we have

p_{Y\#}\Sigma^{j^{*}v}1_{Y}\simeq p_{({\operatorname{HGr}}(r-1,n-1)\#}\Sigma^{v^{\prime}}1_{{\operatorname{HGr}}(r-1,n-1)}

in
{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
.
By the induction hypothesis on
r
, we conclude that
p_{Y\#}\Sigma^{j^{*}v}1_{Y}
belongs to
\langle 1_{k}\rangle
.

Let us now prove the claim for the right-hand term of (
4.3
). Let us recall that
Z
is isomorphic to the tautological rank
2r
symplectic bundle over
{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n-1)
, and as such, it is a smooth
k
-scheme, with a projection
q^{\prime}:Z\to{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n-1)
realizing
Z
as a vector bundle over
{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n-1)
. For simplicity, let us call
X\coloneqq{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)
. By Theorem
2.17
, we have

p_{\#}i_{*}i^{*}\Sigma^{v}1_{X}\simeq p_{Z\#}\Sigma^{N_{i}}i^{*}\Sigma^{v}1_{X}\simeq p_{Z\#}\Sigma^{N_{i}+i^{*}v}1_{Z}.

Since
q^{\prime}
is a vector bundle, there is an element
v^{\prime\prime}\in K_{0}({\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n-1))
such that
q^{\prime*}v^{\prime\prime}=i^{*}v+N_{i}
in
K_{0}(Z)
. Since
q^{\prime}
is an
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-weak equivalence,

p_{Z\#}\Sigma^{N_{i}+i^{*}v}1_{Z}\simeq p_{{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n-1)\#}\Sigma^{v^{\prime\prime}}1_{{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n-1)}.

By our induction hypothesis, we have that
p_{Z\#}\Sigma^{N_{i}+i^{*}v}1_{Z}
belongs to
\langle 1_{k}\rangle
.

Therefore, we conclude that
p_{\#}\Sigma^{V}1_{{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)}
belongs to
\langle 1_{k}\rangle
.
∎

Corollary 4.8
.

The symplectic cobordism spectrum
{\operatorname{MSp}}
is cellular.

Proof.

By definition,
{\operatorname{MSp}}
is isomorphic to the filtered colimit of the system

\Sigma^{\infty}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}{\operatorname{MSp}}_{0}\to\Sigma^{-4,-2}\Sigma^{\infty}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}{\operatorname{MSp}}_{2}\to\Sigma^{-8,-4}\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{\infty}{\operatorname{MSp}}_{4}\to\ldots,

which is in particular a homotopy colimit. Thus, it is enough to show that
\Sigma^{\infty}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}{\operatorname{MSp}}_{2r}
is cellular for all
r
.
{\operatorname{MSp}}_{2r}
is in turn the homotopy colimit
{\operatorname*{colim}}_{n}{\operatorname{Th}}(E_{r,n}^{{\operatorname{Sp}}})
, and
\Sigma^{\infty}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}{\operatorname{Th}}(E_{r,n}^{\operatorname{Sp}})
is cellular for all
r,n
by Proposition
4.7
. This concludes the proof.
∎

4.2.
The motive of
{\operatorname{MSp}}

By adapting the methods of the previous subsection, one can express the cellularity of
{\operatorname{MSp}}
in terms of its motive
{\operatorname{MSp}}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}
. We introduce some notation: for a free graded
{\mathbb{Z}}
-module
{\mathbb{Z}}\cdot x
, with
x
in degree
n
, we write
{\mathbb{Z}}\cdot x\otimes{\mathcal{E}}
for the motivic spectrum
\Sigma^{-2n,-n}{\mathcal{E}}\in{\operatorname{SH}}(k)
. This naturally extends to define
M_{*}\otimes{\mathcal{E}}
for
M_{*}=\oplus_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}M_{n}
a direct sum of free graded
{\mathbb{Z}}
-modules.

Proposition 4.9
.

The motive of
{\operatorname{MSp}}
in
{\operatorname{DM}}(k)
has the form

{\operatorname{MSp}}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}[b^{\prime}_{1},b_{2}^{\prime},\ldots]\otimes H{\mathbb{Z}},

with
b_{i}^{\prime}
a polynomial generator of degree
-2i
.

Proof.

First, we show that for all
n\geq r\geq 0
, the
H{\mathbb{Z}}
-module
\Sigma^{\infty}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)_{+}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}
is of the form
\oplus_{s}\Sigma^{4m_{s},2m_{s}}H{\mathbb{Z}}
, for a finite number of non-negative integers
m_{s}
.

For
r=0
or
r=n
, the statement trivially holds. We can then suppose
n>r>0
and the claim true for all quaternionic Grassmannians
{\operatorname{HGr}}(r^{\prime},n^{\prime})
with
r^{\prime}\leq r
and
n^{\prime}<n
, or
r^{\prime}<r
and
n^{\prime}\leq n
.

We take
Z\coloneqq N^{+}
,
X\coloneqq{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)
with structure map
p
, and
i,j
the respective inclusion as in (
4.1
), and we consider again the localization distinguished triangle (
4.2
). Applying
p_{\#}(-)(1_{X})\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}
to (
4.2
) gives the distinguished triangle

(4.4)

p_{\#}j_{!}j^{!}1_{X}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}\to\Sigma^{\infty}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}X_{+}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}\to p_{\#}i_{*}i^{*}1_{X}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}\xrightarrow{\alpha}j_{!}j^{!}1_{X}[1]\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}

in
{\operatorname{DM}}(k)
. As in the proof of Proposition
4.7
, we have
p_{\#}j_{!}j^{!}1_{X}\simeq p_{Y\#}1_{Y}
and
p_{\#}i_{*}i^{*}1_{X}\simeq p_{Z\#}\Sigma^{N_{i}}1_{Z}
, with
N_{i}
the normal bundle of
i
.

For the remainder of the proof, except where indicated to the contrary, we will work in
{\operatorname{DM}}(k)
.

Let us look at the right-hand term
p_{Z\#}\Sigma^{N_{i}}1_{Z}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}
of (
4.4
). Since
p_{Z\#}
satisfies projection formula against
p_{Z}^{*}
by Remark
2.20
, we have
p_{Z\#}\Sigma^{N_{i}}1_{Z}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq p_{Z\#}\Sigma^{N_{i}}p_{Z}^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}},

and through the isomorphism
{\operatorname{th}}^{f}_{H{\mathbb{Z}}{\operatorname{Mod}}}(N_{i})
, we get
p_{Z\#}\Sigma^{N_{i}}p_{Z}^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Sigma^{4c,2c}p_{Z\#}p_{Z}^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
2c
the codimension of
Z
in
X
. By the projection formula again, we have
p_{Z\#}p_{Z}^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{\infty}Z_{+}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}
, but
Z
is a vector bundle over
{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n-1)
, so
\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{\infty}Z_{+}\simeq\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{\infty}{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n-1)_{+}
. We can then apply the induction hypothesis to get

p_{Z\#}\Sigma^{N_{i}}1_{Z}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq\Sigma^{4c,2c}\oplus_{i}\Sigma^{4m_{i}^{Z},2m_{i}^{Z}}H{\mathbb{Z}}

for a finite number of non-negative integers
m_{i}^{Z}
.

Let us look at the left-hand term
p_{Y\#}1_{Y}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}
of (
4.4
). As in the proof of Proposition
4.7
, there is an
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-weak equivalence
h:{\operatorname{HGr}}(r-1,n-1)\to Y
, then
p_{Y\#}1_{Y}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{\infty}{\operatorname{HGr}}(r-1,n-1)_{+}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}
. We can then use again our induction hypothesis to get

p_{Y\#}1_{Y}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq\oplus_{i^{\prime}}\Sigma^{4m_{i^{\prime}}^{Y},2m_{i^{\prime}}^{Y}}H{\mathbb{Z}}

for a finite number of non-negative integers
m_{i^{\prime}}^{Y}
.

Now, the boundary map
\alpha
in (
4.4
) is an element in

[p_{\#}i_{*}i^{*}1_{X}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}},j_{!}j^{!}1_{X}[1]\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{DM}}(k)}\cong[\Sigma^{4c,2c}\oplus_{i}\Sigma^{4m_{i}^{Z},2m_{i}^{Z}}H{\mathbb{Z}},\Sigma^{1,0}\oplus_{i^{\prime}}\Sigma^{4m_{i^{\prime}}^{Y},2m_{i^{\prime}}^{Y}}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{DM}}(k)}\cong\\
\oplus_{i,i^{\prime}}[\Sigma^{4(c+m_{i}^{Z}),2(c+m_{i}^{Z})}H{\mathbb{Z}},\Sigma^{4m_{i^{\prime}}^{Y}+1,2m_{i^{\prime}}^{Y}}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{DM}}(k)}\cong\\
\oplus_{i,i^{\prime}}[H{\mathbb{Z}},\Sigma^{4(m_{i^{\prime}}^{Y}-m_{i}^{Z}-c)+1,2(m_{i^{\prime}}^{Y}-m_{i}^{Z}-c)}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{DM}}(k)}.

By the free-forgetful adjunction, this corresponds to an element in

\oplus_{i,i^{\prime}}[1_{k},\Sigma^{4(m_{i^{\prime}}^{Y}-m_{i}^{Z}-c)+1,2(m_{i^{\prime}}^{Y}-m_{i}^{Z}-c)}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(k)}=\oplus_{i,i^{\prime}}H{\mathbb{Z}}^{4(m_{i^{\prime}}^{Y}-m_{i}^{Z}-c)+1,2(m_{i^{\prime}}^{Y}-m_{i}^{Z}-c)}({\operatorname{Spec}}k),

but each abelian group
H{\mathbb{Z}}^{4(m_{i^{\prime}}^{Y}-m_{i}^{Z}-c)+1,2(m_{i^{\prime}}^{Y}-m_{i}^{Z}-c)}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)
is trivial by Theorem
2.8
. We conclude that
\alpha=0
, and the sequence (
4.4
) splits. Hence

\Sigma^{\infty}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)_{+}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq\oplus_{s}\Sigma^{4m_{s},2m_{s}}H{\mathbb{Z}}

for a finite number of non-negative integers
m_{s}
, proving the claim.

Now, let us recall that
{\operatorname{BSp}}_{2r}={\operatorname*{colim}}_{N}{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,rN)
. Since the
\text{free}_{H{\mathbb{Z}}}
functor
(-)\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}
is left adjoint, it commutes with small colimits, hence

(4.5)

\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{\infty}{\operatorname{BSp}}_{2r\,+}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq{\operatorname*{colim}}_{N}(\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{\infty}{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,rN)_{+}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}).

Now we claim that, once we fix
r
, for each non-negative integer
m
the number of summands in the right-hand side for which
m_{s}=m
is eventually constant for
n>>0
. We can assume that this is the case for
\Sigma^{\infty}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}{\operatorname{HGr}}(r-1,n)_{+}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}
by induction. Noting that
Z
has codimension
c=2r
, independent of
n
, the above argument for the identity
\Sigma^{\infty}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)_{+}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq\oplus_{s}\Sigma^{4m_{s},2m_{s}}H{\mathbb{Z}}
shows that the pairs
(4m,2m)
that occur for
{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n+1)
are the pairs that occur for
{\operatorname{HGr}}(r-1,n)
together with pairs of the form
(4(r+m^{\prime}),2(r+m^{\prime}))
where
(4m^{\prime},2m^{\prime})
is a pair occurring for
{\operatorname{HGr}}(r,n)
. This proves the claim.

We can then rewrite (
4.5
) as

(4.6)

\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{\infty}{\operatorname{BSp}}_{2r\,+}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq\oplus_{s}\Sigma^{4m_{s},2m_{s}}H{\mathbb{Z}},

where now the sum will be infinite, but for all
m
there is only a finite number of summands for which
m_{s}=m
.

As before, by using projection formula of
p_{{\operatorname{BSp}}_{2r}\#}
against
p_{{\operatorname{BSp}}_{2r}}^{*}
and the isomorphism
{\operatorname{th}}^{f}_{H{\mathbb{Z}}{\operatorname{Mod}}}(E_{2r}^{\operatorname{Sp}}):\Sigma^{E_{2r}^{\operatorname{Sp}}}p_{{\operatorname{BSp}}_{2r}}^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Sigma^{4r,2r}p_{{\operatorname{BSp}}_{2r}}^{*}H{\mathbb{Z}}
, we get

p_{{\operatorname{BSp}}_{2r}\#}\Sigma^{E_{2r}^{\operatorname{Sp}}}1_{{\operatorname{BSp}}_{2r}}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq\Sigma^{4r,2r}p_{{\operatorname{BSp}}_{2r}\#}1_{{\operatorname{BSp}}_{2r}}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}.

Thus, since
\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{\infty}{\operatorname{MSp}}_{2r}\simeq p_{{\operatorname{BSp}}_{2r}\#}\Sigma^{E_{2r}^{\operatorname{Sp}}}1_{{\operatorname{BSp}}_{2r}}
, we can write

\Sigma_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}^{\infty}{\operatorname{MSp}}_{2r}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq\Sigma^{4r,2r}\oplus_{s}\Sigma^{4m_{s},2m_{s}}H{\mathbb{Z}},

with
m_{s}
as in (
4.6
). Since
{\operatorname{MSp}}={\operatorname*{colim}}_{r}(\Sigma^{-4r,-2r}\Sigma^{\infty}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}{\operatorname{MSp}}_{2r})
, we can use again that
(-)\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}
commutes with small colimits and that, for each integer
m
, the number of summands in the right hand side of (
4.6
) such that
m_{s}=m
becomes constant for
r>>0
, to get

(4.7)

{\operatorname{MSp}}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq\oplus_{\alpha}\Sigma^{4n_{\alpha},2n_{\alpha}}H{\mathbb{Z}},

where, for all integers n, there are only finitely many indices
\alpha
with
n_{\alpha}=n
, and each
n_{\alpha}
is non-negative.

We have then the following chain of isomorphisms:

H{\mathbb{Z}}^{*,*}({\operatorname{MSp}})=[{\operatorname{MSp}},\Sigma^{*,*}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(k)}\cong[{\operatorname{MSp}}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}},\Sigma^{*,*}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{DM}}(k)}\\
\overset{\eqref{eqn:MSpDecom}}{\cong}[\oplus_{\alpha}\Sigma^{4n_{\alpha},2n_{\alpha}}H{\mathbb{Z}},\Sigma^{*,*}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{DM}}(k)}\cong\prod_{\alpha}[H{\mathbb{Z}},\Sigma^{*-4n_{\alpha},*-2n_{\alpha}}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{DM}}(k)}\\
\cong\prod_{\alpha}[1_{k},\Sigma^{*-4n_{\alpha},*-2n_{\alpha}}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(k)}=\oplus_{\alpha}H{\mathbb{Z}}^{*,*}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)\cdot b_{\alpha},

with
b_{\alpha}
in bidegree
(4n_{\alpha},2n_{\alpha})
. But from Theorem
2.68
, with Remark
2.69
, we know that
H{\mathbb{Z}}^{*,*}({\operatorname{MSp}})\simeq H{\mathbb{Z}}^{*,*}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)[b_{1},b_{2},\ldots]
,
with
b_{i}
in bidegree
(4i,2i)
, in other words,
H{\mathbb{Z}}^{*,*}({\operatorname{MSp}})
is a free bigraded
H{\mathbb{Z}}^{*,*}
-module with basis the monomials in
b_{1},b_{2},\ldots
. Thus, the bidegrees
(4n_{\alpha},2n_{\alpha})
that occur in our decomposition (
4.7
) are exactly the bidegrees of the monomials in
b_{1},b_{2},\ldots
.

From this we see that

{\operatorname{MSp}}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}[b_{1}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime},\ldots]\otimes H{\mathbb{Z}},

with
b_{i}^{\prime}
a polynomial generator of degree
-2i
.
∎

Corollary 4.10
.

For any integer
m\geq 1
, the canonical map

H{\mathbb{Z}}^{*,*}({\operatorname{MSp}})^{\otimes_{H{\mathbb{Z}}^{*,*}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)}m}\to H{\mathbb{Z}}^{*,*}({\operatorname{MSp}}^{\wedge m})

is an isomorphism. The same holds if we replace
H{\mathbb{Z}}
with
H{\mathbb{Z}}/\ell
for any prime
\ell
.

Proof.

In the proof of Proposition
4.9
we have seen that we can write the motive of
{\operatorname{MSp}}
as

(4.8)

{\operatorname{MSp}}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq\oplus_{\alpha}\Sigma^{4n_{\alpha},2n_{\alpha}}H{\mathbb{Z}},

for some non-negative integer
n_{\alpha}
, and for each non-negative integers
n
, there are only finitely many indices
\alpha
such that
n_{\alpha}=n
.

By induction on
m
, we will also have

(4.9)

({\operatorname{MSp}}^{\wedge m})\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq({\operatorname{MSp}}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}})^{\wedge_{H{\mathbb{Z}}}m}\simeq\oplus_{\beta}\Sigma^{4m_{\beta},2m_{\beta}}H{\mathbb{Z}},

with analogous conditions on the non-negative integers
m_{\beta}
. Thus, computing the motivic cohomology of
{\operatorname{MSp}}^{\wedge m}
we have

(4.10)

H{\mathbb{Z}}^{p,q}({\operatorname{MSp}}^{\wedge m})=[{\operatorname{MSp}}^{\wedge m},\Sigma^{p,q}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(K)}\simeq[{\operatorname{MSp}}^{\wedge m}\wedge H{\mathbb{Z}},\Sigma^{p,q}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{DM}}(k)}\\
\simeq[\oplus_{\beta}\Sigma^{4m_{\beta},2m_{\beta}}H{\mathbb{Z}},\Sigma^{p,q}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{DM}}(k)}\simeq[1_{k},\Sigma^{p-4m_{\beta},q-2m_{\beta}}H{\mathbb{Z}}]_{{\operatorname{SH}}(k)}\\
\simeq\prod_{\beta}H{\mathbb{Z}}^{p-4m_{\beta},q-2m_{\beta}}({\operatorname{Spec}}k).

Since
H{\mathbb{Z}}^{a,b}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)
vanishes for
b<0
by Theorem
2.8
, we also have

(4.11)

\prod_{\beta}H{\mathbb{Z}}^{p-4m_{\beta},q-2m_{\beta}}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)\simeq\oplus_{\beta}H{\mathbb{Z}}^{p-4m_{\beta},q-2m_{\beta}}({\operatorname{Spec}}k).

The expressions (
4.8
) and (
4.9
) give the isomorphism of
H{\mathbb{Z}}
-modules

(\oplus_{\alpha}\Sigma^{4n_{\alpha},2n_{\alpha}}H{\mathbb{Z}})^{\wedge_{H{\mathbb{Z}}}m}\simeq\oplus_{\beta}\Sigma^{4m_{\beta},2m_{\beta}}H{\mathbb{Z}}.

With the product in motivic cohomology, this induces the isomorphism

(\oplus_{\alpha}H{\mathbb{Z}}^{*-4n_{\alpha},*-2n_{\alpha}}({\operatorname{Spec}}k))^{\otimes_{H{\mathbb{Z}}^{*,*}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)}m}\xrightarrow{\sim}\oplus_{\beta}H{\mathbb{Z}}^{*-4m_{\beta},*-2m_{\beta}}({\operatorname{Spec}}k),

which can be rewritten, through (
4.11
) and (
4.10
), as

H{\mathbb{Z}}^{*,*}({\operatorname{MSp}})^{\otimes_{H{\mathbb{Z}}^{*,*}({\operatorname{Spec}}k)}m}\xrightarrow{\sim}H{\mathbb{Z}}^{*,*}({\operatorname{MSp}}^{\wedge m}).

∎

Remark 4.11
.

Since Theorem
2.8
remains true after replacing
H{\mathbb{Z}}
with
H{\mathbb{Z}}/\ell
for any prime
\ell
, the proofs of Proposition
4.9
and Corollary
4.10
also apply after replacing
H{\mathbb{Z}}
with
H{\mathbb{Z}}/\ell.

5.
The Adams spectral sequence for
{\operatorname{MSp}}

From now on, we fix an odd prime
\ell
different from
{\operatorname{char}}k
